**L302**

> The effects of a key item being given: the item, and the record of it.


**L311–315**

> The loot table that drops a key item off its holder. `unique` so it can never be found twice,
>     and gated on the gift not having been made. The holder needs a statblock of their own:

>     guard dropping the seal.


**L329–331**

> The payoff: gear no questline offers. Same shape as `dmkit.items.gear`, and the same four
>     slots — `head`, `cloak`, `ring`, `belt` — which hold nothing the main line was tuned against.


**L338**

> --- doors ------------------------------------------------------------------


**L342–345**

> A door that says in words what it wants. `blockedTextKey` is rendered by
>     `describeRequirement` into the ways-out list, so the reason is on screen before the player
>     tries it.


**L353–355**

> `gate:<id>:open` is the engine's own record and content cannot read it as an objective, so a
> door a quest waits on must also write its own flag. Without it the objective waits on a flag
> nobody writes.


**L369–371**

> The many-bosses door: it takes the proofs and keeps them. `consume: true` is implemented at

**L377**

### modules/shared/dmkit/maps.py


**L1–16**

> Hand-drawn interiors: ASCII art in, a `maps/<id>/` folder out.
>
> A folder is one CSV per layer plus a manifest, and the loader inlines it into `world.maps`
> (packages/module/src/load.ts). A map is drawn as art and expanded here.
>
> Rules the module linter enforces, checked at drawing time instead so the error can point at the art:
>
>   * every layer is the same rectangle, and the first terrain layer has no empty cells;
>   * there must be an `entry` marker, it must be standable, and everything else must be reachable
>     from it;
>   * a gate cell has to sit on door-like terrain.
>
> `Map` names no terrain. `TERRAIN`, `MARKER` and `IMPASSABLE` are class attributes a module must
> supply by subclassing, because a world's terrain vocabulary is its own. See
> `modules/aurendel/src/staticmaps.py`.


**L21–23**

> One hand-drawn interior. Subclass and override the three tables for a world whose terrain
>     vocabulary differs.


**L25–31**

> Declared, never assigned: a subclass must supply all three. An annotation without a value is
> the contract, because these are a world's terrain vocabulary.
>
> TERRAIN    art character -> terrain id. A character not in it is an error, so a typo in the
> art cannot silently become floor.
> MARKER     art character -> marker name, for the cells that carry one.
> IMPASSABLE terrain ids you cannot stand on, to catch drawing mistakes.


**L60–63**

> Fail here rather than in the linter. Furniture is impassable, and a ring of tables will
>         seal a room off as effectively as a wall; catching it at drawing time is the difference
>         between a one-line fix and a rebuild.


**L88**

> Write `<root>/<id>/`. Returns a one-line summary.


**L111**

> Write every map under `root`, in order. Returns the summary lines.


### modules/shared/dmkit/materials.py


**L1–13**

> The vocabulary places are built out of: terrains, palettes, biomes.
>
> Two engine facts shape every module that uses this:
>
>   * `biome.palette` overrides `area.map.palette` (sim/enter.ts), so a settlement cannot borrow its
>     region's biome — every built-up place needs an urban biome of its own or the town renders as the
>     field it stands in.
>   * A POI interior is built by `buildMap` with no palette override (sim/enter.ts), so
>     `poi.map.palette` is honoured. That is what the `int_*` palettes are for.
>
> The four constructors take rows of tuples, so a module's materials file reads as a table.
> `INTERIOR_PALETTES` is the set `dmkit.places.TRADE_PALETTE` names.


**L23–31**

> `(id, name, glyph, colour, passable, opaque, moveCost, description)` tuples into terrain
>     entries. `tags` is tag -> [terrain id]; `extras` is per-id and merged last, so it can
>     override anything derived.
>
>     `marks` is how well ground keeps a trace, as `{group: {sense: 0..1}}` with `group` naming a list
>     in `tags`. Keyed by tag rather than by id because a module has scores of terrains and about four
>     kinds of footing. Anything in no listed group is left absent, which the engine reads as keeping
>     a trace perfectly.


**L60–62**

> `(id, name, floor, wall, door, exterior, scatter)` tuples into palettes. A falsy `door` is
>     left off, for a place with no door.


**L74–76**

> `(id, name, layer, palette, description)` tuples into biomes. Every biome gets an
>     `<id>_ambience` key except those whose id starts with `ambience_prefix`.


**L82–84**

> Ambience fires on entering an area (sim/enter.ts). No area is ever in a dungeon biome — a
> dungeon is reached through a point of interest — so an ambience pool there would be prose
> nothing could read.


### modules/shared/dmkit/places.py


**L1–14**

> Constructors for places — areas, points of interest, and the ways between.
>
> Nothing here names a palette, a text pool or a size. `area()` takes the map it should be, `poi()`
> takes the palette and footprint it should have, and the tables that decide those belong to the
> module, because each is a choice about a world rather than a fact about the engine. See
> `modules/aurendel/src/place.py`.
>
> Two engine facts these do encode:
>
>   * a place narrates from the `narrative.textGrammar` pool named by its `descriptionKey`; the plain
>     `description` is only shown by `look <name>` (narrate.ts);
>   * a generated interior is one undivided walled rectangle (sim/enter.ts sets `rooms: []`), so
>     `poi.map` is outside dimensions — subtract two in each direction for floor.


**L20–22**

> One place with a map. `size` is the `(width, height)` the map should be; `role` is recorded
>     on `extra` and is otherwise the module's business.


**L45–54**

> A building, landmark, or way down.
>
>     `palette` and `footprint` together make an interior; leave both off for something you stand at
>     rather than in, which leaves the party on the area map. A `dungeon` or a `static` map wins over
>     both.
>
>     `desc_key` is required rather than defaulted: which pool an unnamed place falls back on is a
>     decision about a world's prose, and a silently wrong `descriptionKey` is a place that narrates
>     as something else.


**L84**

> A pool many places point at, registered once, first spelling winning.


**L110**

> A way through that wants paying for, and usually can be got round.


### modules/shared/dmkit/prose.py


**L1–11**

> The text-pool registry.
>
> Every place the party can stand narrates from a `narrative.textGrammar` pool
> named by its `descriptionKey`; the plain `description` field is only ever shown
> by `look <name>` (narrate.ts:709). Pools need three or more variants or the
> linter calls them thin.
>
> `pool()` is the only constructor and this module registers nothing itself —
> prose belongs to the world that speaks it. A module imports `pool`, calls it at
> module level, and the build collects the lot with `registered()`.


**L17–22**

> Register a text pool. Later calls with the same id replace earlier ones.
>
>     Last-write-wins is deliberate: it is how a module overrides a pool some
>     shared file registered with prose of its own. `registered()` sorts, so
>     import *order* cannot otherwise reach the built document.


### modules/shared/dmkit/quests.py


**L1–26**

> Quests, dialogue, and the shapes branching actually takes.
>
> The engine has no branch primitive. A quest has one linear stage cursor and one completion test, and
> everything that forks is built out of flags and predicates. These helpers are the four idioms that
> come up:
>
>   * `resolved_either_way` — the branch objectives are `optional`, and one required `custom`
>     objective completes when any branch flag is set. Without it a quest with only optional
>     objectives can never complete, because `checkQuestCompletion` insists on at least one required
>     one.
>   * `either` — an `if` effect, which is how one quest produces two outcomes from the same
>     `onComplete`.
>   * `take_job` — the dialogue option that hands a quest over: gate on the quest being unstarted,
>     `emit: startQuest`, hand over whatever comes with it.
>   * `remembered_as` — put the deed on the dialogue node where the work is turned in, not on the
>     quest, so it is witnessed by the person being reported to.
>
> Traps this file avoids:
>
>   * `ordered` defaults to True, and quest-level objectives sort before stage objectives, so mixing
>     the two under the default forces the loose ones first.
>   * A stage whose objectives are all `optional` is skipped entirely — its `onStart` and `onComplete`
>     never run.
>   * `objective.target` is not a ref, so a typo compiles clean and the objective never fires.
>     `dmkit.lint.objective_targets` is the answer.


**L29**

> --- objectives -----------------------------------------------------------


**L47**

> Arrive somewhere. `target` is a POI id, an area id, or a gate id.


**L52**

> `target` is a monster id — it is matched against `entity.statblock`.


**L65**

> Done when a flag is set — the workhorse for anything the engine has no event for.


**L70–72**

> One required objective satisfied by any of several mutually exclusive outcomes, which is what
>     makes a branch completable.


**L78**

> --- effects --------------------------------------------------------------


**L98**

> One outcome or the other, on a flag. The engine's only branch.


**L106**

> Somebody has had enough of you.


**L110**

> --- quests ---------------------------------------------------------------


**L123**

> Quest rewards are one of two sources of experience, alongside kills.


**L166**

> --- dialogue -------------------------------------------------------------


**L169–173**

> A conversation node.
>
>     A node with no options ends the conversation, so every node that is not meant to be a parting
>     word carries a way out.


**L201–208**

> The quest-giver pattern.
>
>     Gated on the quest being unstarted so it disappears once taken, and the acceptance is an emitted
>     event the reducer picks up before quests advance, so the job can be progressed by the same batch
>     that granted it.
> Merged key by key rather than `update`d: a caller passing its own `quests` clause would
> otherwise replace the unstarted check. Lists concatenate; everything else the caller wins.


**L226–227**

> Somebody who stands where you left them: no wandering, no investigating. They notice everyone,
> though, which is what lets them witness a theft.


**L236**

> Somebody with the run of the place: a guard on a round, a child in a lane.


**L266–267**

> Anyone with a counter to mind or a job to hand out stays put; everyone else has the run of the
> place. Stated rather than left to the ruleset.


### modules/shared/dmkit/regions.py


**L1–19**

> Gathering a continent out of the files that declare pieces of it.
>
> Each module exports whatever it has:
>
>     AREAS      list of area dicts (no `connections` — see EDGES)
>     EDGES      (a, b, minutes) or (a, b, minutes, {...opts}) — written BOTH ways
>     POIS       list of point-of-interest dicts
>     GATES      list of gate dicts
>     DUNGEONS   list of dungeon dicts
>     ROOM_TEMPLATES  list of room-template dicts
>     TRAPS      list of trap dicts
>     BIOME_ROOMS / BIOME_TRAPS   {biome_id: [id, ...]}
>
> Roads are declared once and emitted on both sides, because `world.areas[].connections` is one-
> directional in the engine (reduce.ts). Pass `{"oneWay": True}` when a one-way road is meant.
>
> Everything takes the loaded module list explicitly: the order of that list is the emitted order of
> every collection, so it is the caller's to decide.


**L24–29**

> Import each name, in order, and return the modules.
>
>     No `except ImportError`: a region that fails to import is a third of a continent silently
>     missing from a build that otherwise succeeds. The genuinely-not-built-yet case is handled by
>     `areas()`, loudly.


**L54–59**

> Areas with their connections attached, in declaration order.
>
>     Idempotent: the area dicts are the objects the region files hold, so a second call would append
>     a second copy of every road. It is called more than once — `pois()` asks for them again — so the
>     reset is not optional.


**L68–70**

> Regions can land one at a time while inter-region roads are declared all at once. A road
> to a region that does not exist yet is dropped and reported rather than crashing the
> build.


**L98–101**

> Points of interest, each promoted to its own prose if any was written. A place earns a unique
>     voice by having a `<id>_desc` pool declared somewhere; otherwise it keeps the shared pool for
>     its kind.


**L113–119**

> Give every point of interest a spot on its area's map.
>
>     `position` is where the party stands when they arrive somewhere with no interior, and where the
>     place shows on the map when it has one. The ones that matter are hand-placed and the rest laid
>     out on a grid; `freeNear` (sim/enter.ts) shifts anybody who lands on a wall, so the only hard
>     requirement is that a spot is inside the map.


**L130–131**

> A ring of spots inset from the wall, filled clockwise, then a second ring further in. Two
> rings hold twenty-odd places.


**L154–156**

> Wire each biome's room templates and traps in. Stable and de-duplicated: two regions may
>     contribute to the same biome, and a repeated id is a weighting bug.


### modules/shared/dmkit/retired.py


**L1–13**

> Refuse to overwrite a module that has been handed over to its `project/`.
>
> These generators write `module.json` wholesale. Once a module has a `project/`
> beside it, that tree is the source of truth and `module.json` is built from it
> by `npm run project -- build` — so a generator run would throw away whatever has
> been authored since the handover, and the only thing that would notice is
> `project.test.ts` failing some time later.
>
> Kept rather than deleted because these files are still the only home for what
> was never ported: `lint.py`'s contract checks, the map reachability flood fill,
> the caverns and BSP sizing, the post-game ladder, and two-way road expansion.
> They are how these worlds were first built, not how they are maintained.


**L20**

> Stop unless `--force`, when `out_dir` already has a `project/`.


### modules/shared/dmkit/story.py


**L1–11**

> Gathering a questline out of the files that declare pieces of it.
>
> Each module exports whatever it has:
>
>     NPCS  QUESTS  DIALOGUES  ARCS  LORE  THREADS  ITEMS  GATES
>     LOOT_TABLES  MONSTERS  ENCOUNTER_TABLES
>     POI_TRIGGERS  POI_PATCHES  AREA_ENCOUNTERS  BOSSES
>
> As in `dmkit.regions`, the loaded module list is passed in: its order is the emitted order of every
> collection, so it belongs to the caller.


**L19–23**

> Import each name, in order, and return the modules.
>
>     No `except ImportError`: a chain or thread that quietly fails to import becomes a raft of
>     "nothing starts it" problems in the linter that look like a different bug.


**L35–41**

> Every arc, plus the ending arc if one is asked for.
>
>     `ending` is `(arc_id, name, description, [quest_id, ...])`. Only the quests that exist go in, in
>     the order given. Assembled here rather than in the last act's file because whether a run is over
>     is a fact about the whole questline: `isEnding` plus every quest in it complete is what
>     `endingReached` wins on.


**L53–56**

> Arrival triggers, from the questline and from whichever chains want one. Each module may
>     export its own `POI_TRIGGERS` in the same shape, gathered here so the geography files do not
>     have to know a quest exists.


**L69–77**

> Turn an existing place into a hidden one, or hang a door on it.
>
>     A hidden thread builds no geography, so the only thing it does to the map is patch a place that
>     already exists: `hidden` plus a `discover` difficulty that falls as its thread fills, and a
>     `gate` that says in words what it wants.
>
>     Merged rather than replaced, so a patch cannot silently drop a place's triggers or its
>     residents.


**L92–93**

> Loud, because a patch that lands on nothing is a thread whose anchor is not hidden, not
> gated, and reachable by walking in.


**L97–98**

> Which places a questline turns live. Everything not named here stays as quiet as the geography
> left it.


**L110–119**

> Turn the route live: encounters, loot, and rooms that are not empty.
>
>     Everything is scoped by `population`: a biome, area or dungeon the questline did not name is
>     left as quiet as the geography made it. A module's own `AREA_ENCOUNTERS` and `BOSSES` are
>     gathered alongside, and area tables are appended rather than replaced.
>
>     The three room-encounter chances below exist because `roomTemplate.encounterChance` and
>     `world.generationDefaults.encounterChance` both default to 0, so without them a dungeon full of
>     monsters generates empty.


**L128–130**

> An area's own tables, then whatever a module adds to them. Appended rather than replaced,
> because post-game content claims a place the party already walked has something else in it
> now.


**L143–144**

> Bosses the questline named, then the ones a module claims for itself. A boss room carrying
> `alwaysEncounter` with no table to draw from generates empty.


**L154**

> This is the switch described above.


**L161**

> The boss room draws from the dungeon's `bossTable`, once.


### modules/shared/dmkit/trials.py


**L1–16**

> Post-game content: a ladder of tiers, each gated on the one below.
>
> The same shape as `dmkit.chains`: the head gets the gate and the giver, and every later rung states
> its own `requires` as well as being named in the previous one's `unlocks`, because `unlocks` marks a
> quest available and does not gate starting it.
>
> Two things that make a tier validate perfectly and be unreachable:
>
>   * `start.postVictory` has to be `continue`. Otherwise `settle` sets `outcome: 'victory'` on the
>     ending and returns early ever after — no combat, no encounters, no affordances.
>   * `quest.tags` is inert. The tags written here are for the linter; everything that gates lives in
>     `requires`.
>
> Nothing here looks a gate up: `gate`, `proofs` and `requires` all arrive as arguments, because what
> counts as "the game is finished" is the one fact a shared kit cannot know.


**L21–28**

> The thing a finished tier pays out, and the next tier's key.
>
>     `kind: "key"` and `value: 0` for the reason `dmkit.items.key` gives: `shopStock` skips anything
>     worth nothing, so a quest object cannot be sold by accident.
>
>     An item rather than a flag because a thing in the pack reads better in a journal, and
>     `requires.items` was already there.


**L35**

> One quest of a tier, before `tier()` wires its gating in.


**L41–49**

> A door that wants one of several fabled relics worn.
>
>     `equipped: True` rather than merely carried: what is asked is not whether you own the thing but
>     whether you came dressed for this. `consume` is false — a trial takes time and hit points, never
>     gear.
>
>     `anyOf` over `proofs` rather than a flat `items` list, because insisting on one particular relic
>     would make the door a lottery over which optional content a party happened to do.


**L65–67**

> Wire a tier's quests into an ordered, gated, startable ladder rung. The last rung pays the
>     warrant, which is the next tier's gate.


**L79–80**

> The floor is on the head only, as for a chain: gating every rung on it would make the
> middle of a tier look conditional when it is not.


**L98–111**

> An encounter table for a place already walked, live only behind a gate.
>
>     A tier is not only a new dungeon: places the party has been get encounter groups gated on the
>     same flag, so the quiet half of the map stops being quiet once the ending lands.
>
>     `entries` is a list of `(group_id, [(monster, count, scale), ...], weight)`, and `requires` goes
>     on every group here rather than at each call site, because a group left out of the gating is a
>     monster the party meets in the first act at level three. A group that fails its `requires` is
>     removed from the draw entirely (`world/populate.ts`), so before the gate opens the table
>     collapses to its empty weight.
>
>     Attached to areas rather than biomes, so this stays a list of named places rather than a change
>     to what a whole terrain means.

### packages/authoring/src/chain.ts


**L1–18**

> Wiring a list of quests into a chain that can actually be started.
>
> A chain is four separate pieces of bookkeeping that all have to agree, each failing quietly on
> its own:
>
> - Only the head is offered. Give every link a giver and the whole chain is available at once, out
>   of order.
> - Every later link requires the one before it. Miss one and a chain has two heads, which
>   validates perfectly and plays as two chains.
> - Every link but the last names the next in `unlocks`. This is what `questsAreReachable` follows;
>   without it the tail is unreachable.
> - The level floor goes on the head only, or the middle of a chain looks conditional when it is
>   not.
>
> None of that is checkable after the fact without knowing the author meant a chain, which is what
> this records.

> Anything else the quest carries; merged as written.


**L30**

> What the head waits on — an act gate, a flag, whatever the world uses.

> A level floor, on the head only.


**L36**

> Stamped on every quest, so a rule can assert containment.

> The studio shows the result as a diff before writing it.

> opening is contained rather than leaking. Passed as data rather than guessed, the same way

> — it is run through the real DSL evaluator rather than trusted.
>

> already has a formula, which means the round trip has to hold on all 36 of
> the ones Aurendel ships.

### packages/authoring/src/index.ts


**L1–14**

> Authoring-time algorithms.
>
> The four things a generator has to get right for a module to *play* the way
> it reads, and which no amount of schema validation can check. Each one was a
> silent failure in a shipped world before it was a function.
>
> They live outside `@dm/module` deliberately: `fit` has to agree with the
> engine about spacing, so it calls the engine's own `diceMean` rather than a
> second implementation of it — and a module that depended on the engine would
> be a cycle. They live outside the studio just as deliberately: they are ours,
> there are four of them, and as code they get types and tests rather than
> being user script in a sandbox.

### packages/authoring/src/layout.ts


**L1–14**

> Putting every point of interest somewhere on its area's map.
>
> `position` is where the party stands when they arrive at a place with no
> interior, and where it shows on the area map when it has one. Hand-placing
> four hundred of them is not a good use of anybody; hand-placing the ones that
> matter and laying out the rest is.
>
> The only hard requirement is that a spot is inside the map — `freeNear`
> shifts anyone who lands on a wall — so this optimises for legibility rather
> than correctness: a ring inset from the edge, filled clockwise, then a second
> ring further in. Two rings hold twenty-odd places, which is more than any one
> settlement has.

**L38–48**

> Spots for the entries that have none.
>
> Returns placements rather than mutating, so the caller decides what to do
> with them — the studio shows them as a diff before applying, because a
> hundred positions appearing without warning is not a generator, it is an
> accident.
>
> An entry that already has a position keeps it, and is not returned. That is
> what makes this safe to re-run: hand placement always wins.

> The `index`-th spot on a set of concentric rings inside a `width`×`height` map.

### packages/authoring/src/standing.test.ts


**L1–8**

> The clamp is the design, so it is what the tests are about.
>
> `standingDc` builds a requirement expression, and `dcAt` predicts what it
> comes to — two implementations of one rule, which is a liability unless
> something proves they agree. The DSL evaluator settles it: the expression is
> run for real, at a range of standings, against `dcAt`'s answer.


**L43**

> Not a rounding bug to fix: this is what every shipped standing check


**L44**

> already does, and 19.4 means the roll has to be 20.


**L49**

> The preview and the expression are one rule; this is what keeps them one.


### packages/authoring/src/standing.ts


**L1–12**

> A difficulty that moves with what a faction thinks of you.
>
> At standing 0 the check is `base`. Every `per` points moves it a point in
> your favour, to a maximum of `span` either way — so the whole range is
> `base ± span`.
>
> The clamp is the design, not an implementation detail. No amount of goodwill
> makes a hard thing automatic and no amount of hostility makes it impossible,
> because a gate is shut until you have the key and a *person* is merely
> harder. There is always a roll.


**L15**

> The most the check can move in either direction.


**L17**

> Points of standing per point of difficulty.


**L21**

> A `requirement` DSL expression, ready to be a `check.dc`.


**L37–46**

> What the check actually comes to at a given standing — for a preview.
>
> Fractional between steps, and deliberately so: the DSL's `div` is real
> division, not integer division, so a standing of -27 against `per: 5` gives
> 19.4 rather than 19. That is what the shipped expressions already do, and
> this exists to predict them rather than to improve on them — a preview that
> rounded would disagree with the roll. Worth knowing when showing a DC to
> anybody: 19.4 needs a 20.

### packages/core/src/dice.ts


**L1–16**

> Dice notation: parsing and rolling.
>
> Grammar (whitespace insensitive, case insensitive):
>
>   expr  := term (('+' | '-') term)*
>   term  := dice | integer
>   dice  := [count] 'd' sides [keep]
>   keep  := ('kh' | 'kl') [n]        keep highest / lowest n, default 1
>
> Examples: `1d20`, `d6`, `2d6+3`, `4d6kh3`, `2d20kh1` (advantage), `1d8+1d4-1`.
>
> Parsing is separated from rolling so content can be validated at load time — a typo in a
> monster's damage string is a load error, not a crash mid-combat. Rolls return every individual
> face because the combat log narrates them.


**L22**

> Keep the highest or lowest `keepCount` dice; null keeps all of them.


**L24**

> -1 when the term was subtracted.


**L41**

> One die's outcome, including dice dropped by a keep clause.


**L51**

> Sum of the constant terms, for rendering "17 (14 + 3)".


**L63**

> Guard rails against content that would hang or overflow the roller.


**L69–71**

> Parse dice notation into a reusable expression. Throws {@link DiceParseError} on malformed input.


**L76**

> Split on +/- while keeping the operators. A leading sign is allowed.


**L123**

> Minimal RNG surface the roller needs, so callers can pass any compatible generator.


**L128**

> Roll a parsed expression.


**L146**

> Sort indices by value, stable on index so ties drop deterministically.


**L166**

> Parse and roll in one step. Prefer caching the parse in hot paths.


**L171**

> Lowest possible total. Used to sanity-check content and to bound AI search.


**L176**

> Highest possible total.


**L190**

> A negated term inverts which extreme produces the bound.


**L197**

**L208**

**L209**

> balance tooling by scaling the mean.


### packages/core/src/hash.ts


**L1–11**

> Content tags.
>
> `hash64` is the recipe the module hash has always used, lifted here so the
> mod format can share it rather than keep a second copy that drifts. Two
> 32-bit FNV-1a passes over different salts give a 64-bit hex tag.
>
> **This is change detection, not security.** It identifies a version; it
> authenticates nothing. A mod is arbitrary code, and the only thing standing
> between a hostile mod and the player is the sandbox — never this tag.


**L15–23**

> The separator between the text and its salt.
>
> It is a NUL byte, not a space. The original `hashModule` had the byte written
> *literally* into its source, which is why `packages/module/src/compile.ts`
> still reads as binary to `grep`. Every module hash recorded in every save
> depends on this exact byte, so it is built here with `fromCharCode` rather
> than pasted in — same hash, and this file stays plain ASCII.


**L26**

> A 64-bit content tag as 16 lowercase hex characters.


**L33–37**

> Stable stringify: object keys sorted, so a hash tracks content rather than
> whichever order an editor happened to serialize. `undefined` values are
> dropped, array order is preserved because it is meaningful.


### packages/core/src/ids.ts


**L1–7**

> Entity identity.
>
> Ids must be deterministic: `Date.now()` or a random UUID would break replay,
> since a re-run has to produce byte-identical state. Runtime entity ids come
> from a counter that lives in the save file.


**L9**

> Branded string ids, so a monster id cannot be passed where an item id belongs.


**L12**

**L14**

> A runtime entity spawned during play, e.g. `e:42`.


**L25–29**

> Monotonic entity id allocator. The counter is serialized with the game state
> so a reloaded save keeps issuing ids where it left off and never collides
> with an entity already in the world.


**L51**

> Valid content id: dot-separated lowercase segments, e.g. `items.iron_sword`.


### packages/core/src/noise.test.ts


**L1–7**

> The property that matters is not randomness — it is *correlation*.
>
> Independent per-tile rolls are perfectly random and produce confetti. These
> tests pin the thing that makes a field usable for terrain: neighbours agree,
> distant tiles do not, and the same seed says the same thing forever.


**L38**

> The whole reason this file exists. Neighbouring tiles must be far more


**L39**

> alike than distant ones, or thresholding it produces speckle.


**L68**

> Truncating toward zero rather than flooring would mirror the lattice


**L69**

> about x=0, giving a visible seam down the middle of any map.


**L74**

> And it is still smooth across the boundary.


**L78**

> A golden vector: if the algorithm is ever changed, every generated world


**L79**

> changes with it, and that must be a deliberate act rather than a surprise.


### packages/core/src/noise.ts


**L1–10**

> Value noise, in integer arithmetic.
>
> The point of a noise field is spatial correlation: neighbouring tiles get similar values and
> distant ones unrelated values. That is the difference between a lake and a scattering of puddles,
> and no independent roll per tile can produce it.
>
> Every step is integer arithmetic with one division at the end. Doubles represent all of these
> exactly, so the field is bit-identical on every platform.


**L13**

> Tiles across one lattice cell. Larger means broader features.


**L15**

> Layers of detail, each half the scale and (by `persistence`) quieter.


**L17**

> Amplitude decay per octave, in 1/256ths. 128 is a half.


**L22**

> The field at a tile, in `[0, 1)`.


**L30**

> The splitmix32 finalizer, used here as an integer hash rather than a stream.


**L41–45**

> The lattice value at an integer corner, as a 24-bit integer. Hashed rather than stored, so a
> field costs nothing to create and can be sampled anywhere — negative coordinates included —
> without allocating a grid.


**L51–54**

> Smoothstep in 8-bit fixed point: `3t² − 2t³`, with `t` in `[0, 256)`. Linear interpolation
> between lattice corners leaves visible creases; this rounds them off.


**L59–62**

> Blend two 24-bit values by an 8-bit fraction. `Math.floor(… / 256)` rather than `>> 8`: the
> product reaches 0xFFFFFF × 255, and a shift would coerce that to int32 and wrap it negative.


**L67**

> One octave, sampled at an integer tile with a given lattice size.


**L69**

> `Math.floor` rather than `| 0`, which truncates toward zero and would fold the lattice back on


**L70**

> itself either side of the origin.


**L74**

> Position within the cell, as a fraction in [0, 256).


**L83–87**

> A deterministic value-noise field. Seeded by a plain integer rather than an `Rng`: the caller
> derives a throwaway sub-stream and takes one number from it, so sampling cannot advance or depend
> on the run's generator.


**L93**

> Amplitudes in 1/256ths, and the total to divide by, both fixed up front so `at` is a pure sum


**L94**

> of integers with a single division at the end.


**L105**

> Once an octave contributes nothing, the rest contribute nothing either.


**L109**

> 24-bit corners × the summed amplitude, so the quotient lands in [0, 1).

### packages/core/src/rng.ts


**L1–10**

> Deterministic, serializable random number generation.
>
> Every random decision in the game flows through here. `Math.random()` must never appear in the
> engine: a save stores RNG state verbatim, so seed plus action log reproduces a run exactly, and
> that only holds if this is the sole source of chance.
>
> Algorithm is xoshiro128** — four 32-bit words of state, which serializes to four integers rather
> than an opaque blob.


**L12**

> Serializable RNG state: exactly four 32-bit words.


**L21–24**

> splitmix32 — used only to expand a single seed into well-distributed state. xoshiro recovers
> poorly from low-entropy seeds, so it is never seeded directly.


**L38**

> FNV-1a. Turns a stream label into a 32-bit value for seed derivation.


**L61**

> Create a generator from a single integer seed.


**L65**

> All-zero state is xoshiro's one fixed point; it would emit zeroes forever.


**L70**

> Create a generator from a textual seed, so "grimhold" is a valid seed.


**L75**

> Restore an exact generator from serialized state.


**L80–88**

> Derive an independent sub-stream.
>
> Sub-streams are why combat rolls cannot shift dungeon layout: the dungeon draws from
> `dungeon:<siteId>` while a fight draws from `combat:<encounterId>`.
>
> Reads the parent's state without advancing it, so deriving is itself deterministic and side-
> effect free.


**L94**

> Snapshot state for saving.


**L99**

> Independent copy at the current position; advancing one will not affect the other.


**L104**

> Core step: a uniform 32-bit unsigned integer.


**L119**

> Uniform float in [0, 1).


**L124–127**

> Uniform integer in [min, max], inclusive. Rejection sampling rather than modulo: modulo bias is

**L138**

> Discard the values in the final, incomplete bucket.


**L145**

### packages/engine/src/creation.ts


**L1–13**

> What a legal starting character is.
>
> `start.creation` declares a point budget, a cost table, and which ancestries
> and classes a campaign allows — and every one of those was enforced only by
> the CLI and web creation screens. Any other way of making a party (a test, a
> `defaultChoices` call, a third-party front end) walked straight past them, so
> the rules were a property of one user interface rather than of the game.
>
> They live here now, beside `createCharacter`, which is the one door every
> character comes through. The front ends still own *how* a player spends the
> budget; this owns whether the result is legal.


**L25–30**

> Total cost of a score.
>
> The module's table is cumulative — the cost of *having* that score, not of
> the last step — because that is how point-buy tables are written.


**L38**

> Outside the table: continue at the rate of its last step, so a module


**L39**

> that only tabulates 8–15 still prices a 16 — and prices it like a 15,


**L40**

> not like a rounding error. Never negative: dumping a score below the


**L41**

> table's floor gives nothing back, or a module with a cheap floor would


**L42**

> hand out free points.


**L54**

> No table: one point per step above the declared default.


**L58**

> What an allocation costs in total.


**L70**

> A starting allocation: everything at its declared default.


**L79–86**

> Why this character could not be made, or null.
>
> Returns a reason rather than throwing so a creation screen can show it while
> `createCharacter` turns it into an error — one rule, two ways of meeting it.
> An empty `allowedAncestries` or `allowedClasses` means no restriction, which
> is what the schema's default of `[]` says.


### packages/engine/src/events.ts

> The engine never produces prose. It produces these, and the narrator turns them into text, so the

>
> Two halves of the same roll, both of which were stuck. `finesse` shipped as
> an item property with an empty body, because `itemProperties[].modifiers`
> maps to derived stats and finesse is not a bonus to anything -- it is a
> *choice* of which attribute the roll uses, and no number added to a defence
> can mean "use agility instead of might".
>
> Meanwhile a weapon attack added the bare attribute modifier and nothing
> else, and nothing raises an attribute after character creation, so a warden
> hit exactly as often at level 20 as at level 1. Spells had
> `spellcasting.attackBonus`; weapons had no equivalent at all.

## `packages/engine/src/grid`

_106 comments_


### packages/engine/src/grid/fov.ts


**L1–13**

> Field of view, by recursive shadowcasting.
>
> This runs for every creature that needs to perceive, every turn. Casting a ray to every tile in
> range is O(r³) and produces artefacts — pillars that leak light, walls with holes; recursive
> shadowcasting is O(r²) and has none.
>
> It matters beyond drawing the map: `witness.requiresLineOfSight` is answered here, so whether an
> NPC learns what the party did comes down to this computation.
>
> The map is divided into eight octants and each is scanned outward row by row. Each recursion
> carries a slope range; a wall narrows it, and when the range closes the scan ends.


**L19**

> Multipliers that transform octant-local coordinates into map coordinates.


**L31**

> The set of tiles visible from a point, as packed keys.


**L41–44**

> Compute visible tiles from `origin`. The origin is always visible, and opaque tiles are
> themselves visible — you can see the wall, you just cannot see past it.


**L78**

> Slopes to the leading and trailing edges of this tile.


**L90**

> Euclidean, so the visible area is a disk. Chebyshev would make it a square, letting a


**L91**

> creature see noticeably further along the diagonals.


**L107**

> Start a shadow: scan the sector to this wall's left, then continue past it with a


**L108**

> narrowed range.


**L117–126**

> Whether an unobstructed line exists between two tiles.
>
> Used for targeting and for witnessing, where computing a whole field of view would be wasteful.
> Endpoints are exempt: a creature standing in a doorway can still be seen and can still see out.
>
> Symmetric by construction. Bresenham breaks ties by direction, so drawing a line one way can clip
> a corner the other way does not — which would let one creature shoot another that cannot shoot
> back. Ordering the endpoints canonically before tracing removes that.


**L139**

> Walk the line and stop at the first opaque tile between the ends.


**L147**

> Local Bresenham, kept here so line of sight has no import cycle.


**L173–177**

> Tiles lit by any light source, for describing a dark room. Light is a union of fields of view,
> one per source, which is why a torch around a corner does not illuminate the corridor you are
> standing in.


### packages/engine/src/grid/geometry.ts


**L1–11**

> Grid geometry: distance, lines, and area shapes.
>
> Distance is Chebyshev — diagonal movement costs the same as orthogonal. It is the convention
> tabletop games use for a square grid, it keeps circles from looking like diamonds, and it means
> "within 6" is a square of side 13. Euclidean distance is available where a genuinely round shape
> is wanted.
>
> Lines are Bresenham — integer-only and the same routine for line of sight, line-shaped spells and
> thrown objects, so a spell can never reach somewhere the eye cannot see it.


**L16**

> Chebyshev distance: the number of steps when diagonals are free.


**L21**

> True Euclidean distance, for genuinely circular areas.


**L28**

> Manhattan distance, for modules that want orthogonal-only movement.


**L37–40**

> Every tile on the line from `from` to `to`, inclusive of both. Bresenham's algorithm; the order
> matters, because callers walk it outward from the origin to find the first blocking tile.


**L68**

> All tiles within `radius` Chebyshev steps, including the centre.


**L79**

> The ring of tiles at exactly `radius` steps.


**L92**

> Area shapes an ability can project, matching `areaOfEffect.shape`.


**L97**

> Radius, side, or length — in tiles.


**L99**

> Where it is centred, or where a cone or line starts.


**L101**

> Direction for cones and lines; ignored by the rest.


**L103**

> Half-angle of a cone, in degrees. Defaults to the quarter-circle blast.


**L105**

> Width of a line shape.


**L109–113**

> The tiles an area covers. Shapes are computed geometrically and then filtered by what can
> actually be reached, since a fireball does not curve around a corner. The caller supplies that
> filter, because the line-of-sight check needs the map and this module does not have it.


**L120**

> A cube of side `size` centred on the origin.


**L125**

> Round in the plane; a cylinder differs only in a dimension the grid does not model.


**L129**

> Centred on the caster and excluding their own tile.


**L143**

> Widen by taking every tile within half the width of the spine.


**L163**

> A tile is in the cone when it is within range and its bearing from the origin is within the


**L164**

> cone's half-angle — 45 degrees each side unless the ability says otherwise. A small


**L165**

> tolerance keeps the edges from looking ragged.


**L186**

> Unit vector from `from` toward `to`; zero-length defaults to east.


**L195–198**

> The eight-way compass direction from one tile to another, as a `systemText` key. Geometry decides
> which octant; the module decides what to call it.


**L213**

> Step one tile from `from` toward `to`, diagonals included.

### packages/engine/src/grid/path.ts


**L1–12**

> Pathfinding: A* over the tile map.
>
> Runs for every creature that moves, every turn, so it uses a binary heap rather than scanning an
> array for the cheapest node.
>
> Movement cost comes from the module: each terrain declares a `moveCost` and each movement mode a
> `terrainMultiplier`, so a swimmer crosses water cheaply and a walker cannot cross it at all.
>
> Ties are broken by insertion order, so the same request always returns the same path — a replay
> that diverged because two routes tied would be hard to debug.


**L19**

> A minimal binary heap keyed on cost, stable on insertion order.


**L64**

> Insertion order breaks ties, which is what keeps paths reproducible.

> Movement modes the mover has, e.g. `["walk", "swim"]`.


**L82**

> Tiles that cannot be entered — other creatures, usually.


**L84**

> Give up after this much accumulated cost.


**L86**

> Whether diagonal steps are allowed.


**L88–91**

> Stop on reaching any tile adjacent to the goal rather than the goal itself — what a melee
> attacker wants, since the target's own tile is occupied.


**L96**

> Tiles from the step after `from` through the destination. Empty if none.


**L104**

> Find the cheapest path, or {@link NO_PATH} when none exists within budget.


**L137**

> The goal itself may be occupied — the normal case when moving to attack — so an occupied


**L138**

> goal is still worth reaching.


**L152**

> Chebyshev heuristic matches the movement model, so A* stays admissible.

> Every tile reachable within a movement budget, with what it costs to get there. Draws the "where
> can I move" highlight, and tells an AI where it can stand.


**L223–226**

> Flood fill of everything connected to a start tile. Used by generation to prove a dungeon has no
> sealed-off rooms.


### packages/engine/src/grid/tiles.ts


**L1–9**

>
> A map is a flat array of terrain ids with a width — flat rather than nested because it serializes
> compactly into a save and indexes without allocating.
>

> terrain definitions, so the same map means different things under different rulesets.

> A rectangular map of terrain ids. Serializable as-is.


**L22**

> `width * height` terrain ids, row-major.


**L26**

> Terrain properties, as the module declares them.

> number because whether ground takes a print and whether it holds a smell are different
> questions.

**L51**

> Bounded by map size; 16 bits per axis is far more than any playable map.

> Terrain id at a position; empty string when out of bounds.


**L69**

> Build a map filled with one terrain.

> Draw the outline of a rectangle.


**L133–136**

> Terrain lookup for a compiled module. Built once per module rather than per query: movement and
> field of view read terrain properties for every tile they touch.


**L140**

> `movementModes[].terrainMultiplier`, by mode id.


**L166**

> Out-of-bounds and unknown terrain read as solid rock: impassable and opaque. Treating the


**L167**

> unknown as open would let creatures walk off the edge of the world.


**L188–192**

> How much of a trace this ground keeps for one sense, from zero to one. One when the terrain

**L210–220**

> Movement cost to enter, or `Infinity` when the tile cannot be entered.
>
> The terrain's own cost times the mover's cheapest mode multiplier, per
> `movementModes[].terrainMultiplier`. A creature with several modes uses whichever crosses this
> ground best.
>
> "Best of the modes it has", not "best of those and also 1": a running minimum starting at 1
> would mean a multiplier above 1 could never win, and a mode declared as worse over some ground
> would be free.

**L260–264**

> would be a cycle.

## `packages/engine/src/mods`

_82 comments_


### packages/engine/src/mods/hooks.ts


**L1–19**

> The hook contract: the whole surface a mod attaches to.
>
> Deliberately small. Every hook is a place the engine already had a seam, and each costs a call
> site in a hot function, so they are added when something needs them.
>
> Why hooks rather than patching functions
>
> Both front ends are browsers and packages are bundled from source, so there is no module registry
> to reach into and ESM live bindings are read-only: a mod cannot literally replace `applyOps`.
> Declared hook points with before / after / replace semantics give the same power while leaving
> one place where mod output enters the engine.
>
> What a mod may do
>
> Anything. `replace` on `action.before` skips the core handler outright, and the `patch` directive
> writes anywhere in `GameState`. The runtime enforces determinism and containment, and nothing
> else.


**L26**

> Before the action switch. `replace` skips the core handler entirely.


**L28**

> After the action switch, before the rng is written back.


**L30–34**

> An effect op the engine does not implement. The branch already existed to refuse unknown ops,
> so a mod can add a genuinely new effect op — usable from module JSON, editable in the studio —
> with no core change.


**L36**

> An occasion firing, before the module's own triggers run.


**L38–44**

> After a reduction settles: perception, combat start/stop, AI turns, and the victory and defeat
> checks have all run.
>
> No `replace`. `settle` enforces the invariants that keep a game coherent. A mod that wants to
> stand in for them can do it from `action.before` with `replace`, where the intent is explicit.


**L46**

> After the world clock advances, once per call rather than per minute.


**L48**

> Whether a module trigger fires. `replace` decides it outright.


**L50**

> After an entity's passive traits and item procs are evaluated.


**L52**

> A creature's chance to react, alongside its statblock's own reactions.


**L54–58**

> Every event, as it is emitted. Fires hundreds of times a turn, so a declaration must carry a
> `match` naming the event type — an unfiltered one would put a WASM crossing on every event in
> the game.


**L74**

> Hooks that must be narrowed, because unfiltered they would be ruinous.


**L81**

> World facts every hook gets. Cheap to build, and usually enough on its own.


**L124–128**

> The narrowing key for a hook, matched against a declaration's `match`. This is what keeps the
> boundary crossing rare: a mod declaring `applyOp` with `match: "gainMomentum"` is only consulted
> for that op.


**L149**

> Nothing sensible to narrow on: these fire once per reduction, which is rare enough that a


**L150**

> crossing per call is affordable.


**L157**

> What a hook run did, so the call site knows whether to run the core path.


**L159**

> A `replace` handler stood in for the core implementation.


**L161**

> A handler refused the action.

### packages/engine/src/mods/runtime.ts


**L1–14**

> Where mod output enters the engine.
>
> One object, two responsibilities:
>
>   - `has()` is the hot-path gate: a `Set` lookup over what the manifests declared, so a turn with
>     no interested mod costs no allocation, serialization, or boundary crossing.
>   - `run()` crosses into the sandbox and applies whatever comes back. It never throws — a mod
>     that explodes, hangs, or returns nonsense becomes a `modError` event and the turn continues.
>
> Mods are not restricted in what they change; `patch` writes anywhere in `GameState`. What is
> enforced here is that values are JSON-safe so a replay reproduces, and that a failure does not
> take the session down.


**L34–38**

> Composite map keys. Each goes through one helper rather than being inlined at both ends: a `${a}
> ${b}` written twice is a key that agrees only by luck, and when the spellings drift the lookup
> silently misses.


**L40**

> Stands for "this declaration did not narrow"; cannot collide with a real match.


**L53**

> Position in the resolved order; the deterministic tiebreak.


**L59**

> In resolved order. Order is behaviour, so it is decided before we get here.


**L61**

> Mods whose `replace` on a given hook lost, as `<modId> <hook>`.


**L67**

> hook name → registrations, pre-sorted into run order.


**L69**

> `${hook} ${match}` and `${hook} ` — what `has()` probes.


**L71**

> A mod's prose is the mod author's to write.


**L73–77**

> Declarations refused when the runtime was built, with the reason. Kept rather than thrown: one
> bad declaration costs a mod that hook, not the whole session, and a silently ignored
> declaration is the most confusing outcome.


**L91**

> `event.emit` runs hundreds of times a turn, so an unfiltered declaration would put a WASM


**L92**

> crossing on every event in the game. Refused outright; a mod author who wants everything


**L93**

> can declare the types they mean.


**L111**

> An unfiltered declaration has to answer for every subject, so it registers the bare key


**L112**

> too.


**L118**

> Run order: before, then replace, then after; within a mode by priority descending, then by


**L119**

> resolved position. Fixed here so `run` never sorts.


**L130**

> Is any mod interested? A `Set` lookup, called on every hooked path.


**L137**

> Run every interested handler and apply what they return. Never throws.


**L163**

> Derived, so how many times a mod draws cannot perturb anything downstream: `derive` reads


**L164**

> the parent without advancing it.


**L216–219**

> Apply one directive. Returns whether it refused the action. Everything goes through the
> transaction, so a mod's changes land the same way the module's own do.


**L223**

> Through the same door the module DSL uses: already validated, clamped, immunity-checked,


**L224**

> and refusing by name on anything unknown.


**L255**

> Resolved here and emitted as text rather than as a key: the narrator does not know which


**L256**

> mod a key belongs to, and two mods may use the same key name.


**L263**

> A missing key shows the key rather than nothing, so an author sees which one they


**L264**

> forgot.


**L275**

> Resolved against the mod's own `systemText`, not the engine's. The engine's key union is


**L276**

> closed — `spine.test.ts` fails on a literal string in engine code — but that rule exists


**L277**

> so engine prose stays authorable, and a mod's prose is the mod author's.


**L345–354**

> Pull one value by dotted path.
>
> Pull rather than push: serializing `GameState` for every hook would be tens to hundreds of KB
> per crossing. A mod asks for the handful of fields it needs.
>
> Two roots. A bare path walks `GameState`. A `module.` prefix reaches the compiled module, and
> `module.<section>.<collection>.<id>` resolves through the id index rather than walking the
> array.


**L360**

> `module.rules.conditions.bleeding` → the entry, not the array.


**L391**

> Immutably set a value at a path, creating plain objects as needed.


**L407**

> Immutably remove a key at a path.

## `packages/engine/src/narrate`

_91 comments_


### packages/engine/src/narrate/grammar.ts


**L1–9**

> Prose from templates.
>
> With no LLM at runtime, variety comes from weighted pools keyed on world state. Expansion is
> seeded per scene rather than per call, so re-describing the same room gives the same sentence
> while two playthroughs describe it differently.
>
> Templates interpolate `{placeholder}` from a context the caller supplies.


**L29**

> Values for `{placeholder}` substitution.


**L31**

> Scope for evaluating variant conditions.


**L33–36**

> Stable identity for the thing being described. The same key always yields the same phrasing
> within a run.


**L41**

> Substitute `{name}` placeholders, leaving unknown ones visible.


**L48**

> An unresolved placeholder is left in place rather than blanked, so a missing value is visible


**L49**

> in play instead of leaving a hole in a sentence.


**L54–57**

> Expand a text pool into a line. Returns an empty string when the pool does not exist, so a
> missing `textKey` degrades to silence rather than throwing mid-scene.


**L67**

> The scene key decides the phrasing, so the same room reads the same way every time it is


**L68**

> described within one run.


**L94**

> A variant whose condition cannot be evaluated is skipped rather than crashing the scene; the


**L95**

> pool's other phrasings still work.


**L101–105**

> The words a sentence is assembled from, resolved from the module once. The rules stay here —
> where the conjunction goes, that a vowel takes the other article — and only the words live in the
> module.


**L116**

> Number words from zero upward; past the end, digits are used.


**L120**

> How a noun reads when there is more than one of it.


**L125**

> Join a list into readable prose: "a, b and c".


**L139–142**

> "a sword" / "an apple". The vowel test is a rule about how the language works; which word each
> branch produces is the module's business.


**L147**

> "three hounds" for small numbers, "12 hounds" beyond.


**L158–167**

> How well a typed noun matches a name, from 100 down to 0 for no match.
>
> The order of the rungs is load-bearing: a whole word beats the start of a longer word, which is
> the difference between `enter mill` finding The Old Mill and finding Millford Village. Putting
> the plain substring test above the word test makes the word test dead code.
>
> Lives here rather than in either front end because the parser and the narrator must agree about
> what a name means.


**L182–186**

> How a creature reads in prose: "a lean, mud-slicked bog hound", from `monsters[].descriptors`.
> Picked with a scene-seeded generator, so the same hound reads the same way each time it is
> mentioned and differently between playthroughs.


### packages/engine/src/narrate/narrate.test.ts


**L1–10**

> The narrator — the layer that turns events into sentences.
>
> The engine emits `Event[]` and never prose, which is what lets a browser and
> a template swap places without touching a rule. That promise only holds while
> this layer keeps two properties: the numbers behind a roll stay visible, and
> a scene reads the same way every time it is described within a run. Both are
> pinned below, along with the grammar helpers that take their words from the
> module and only their placement from here.


**L27**

> A party built the way a new game builds one.


**L74**

> A place must read the same way every time it is described.


**L98**

> The words come from the module; only where they go is the engine's.


### packages/engine/src/narrate/narrate.ts


**L1–10**

> Events into prose.
>
> The engine never writes a sentence; it emits facts, and this turns them into lines. Nothing
> narrated can influence a rule, which is what lets the same run drive a terminal, a browser, or a

### packages/engine/src/narrate/systemText.ts


**L1–12**

> The engine's own words, looked up rather than written.
>
> Every sentence the engine produces lives in `narrative.systemText` and is reached through here.
> There is no English fallback: a key that resolves to nothing throws, because a silent empty
> string is the failure this mechanism exists to prevent. Nothing reaches that throw through a
> normal load — `compileModule` proves every fragment is present and every message keeps its
> placeholders.
>
> A message may be a plain string or `{ pool }`, which hands it to `narrative.textGrammar` for
> weighted, condition-gated variation.


**L19**

> Values for a message's `{placeholders}`.


**L22–31**

> A sentence the engine wants said, carried as a key and its facts.
>
> Events hold one of these rather than finished prose, so what the player reads is decided by the
> module at render time and the same event can render into a terminal, a browser, or a different
> language.
>
> The `text` form is for prose the module already wrote — a dialogue option's `lockedHint`, say —
> which is authored content passing through and needs no key.


**L36**

> Build a message. Shorter than the object literal at ninety-odd call sites.


**L41**

> Carry authored content through as-is.


**L50**

> Only reachable if a module was assembled without compiling it, which the loaders make


**L51**

> impossible. Naming the key beats rendering a blank.


**L58–61**

> Render one message. `sceneKey` gives a pool-backed message a stable identity, so the same refusal
> about the same door reads the same way each time within a run.


**L77**

> Render a `Message` carried on an event.


**L88–90**

> Several messages as one readable phrase, for a refusal that has to name a list inside a sentence.


**L100–103**

> The module's own words for joining, counting, and articles. Built once per module: the narrator
> runs several times a turn and these never change.

### packages/engine/src/newgame.ts

> Starting a game.

> `newGame` is the one place a {@link GameState} is created from nothing. It

## `packages/engine/src/rules`

_150 comments_


### packages/engine/src/rules/apply.ts


**L1–10**

> Applying effects: the bridge from the DSL to game state.
>
> Content describes what should happen as data, `evalEffects` turns that into `EffectOp[]`, and
> this turns those into state changes and events.
>
> An op is a request, and this validates it before applying: the dead take no damage, resources
> clamp to their bounds, immune creatures shrug off conditions, and unknown ids are refused rather
> than crashing. A module cannot corrupt a save.


**L24**

> Type-only: `runtime.ts` imports `applyOps` from here, so a value import would close a cycle.


**L27**

> Accumulates state changes and the events describing them.


**L32–36**

> Depletions currently unwinding, as `<entity>:<resource>`. Per-call scratch, not on `GameState`:
> a module whose `onDepleted` pushes the same pool back to its floor would otherwise recurse
> without end. Never serialized.


**L39–42**

> Guards `event.emit` against re-entering itself: a mod that emits from inside an emit hook would
> recurse until the stack gave out. Per-call scratch, never serialized.


**L48–52**

> Installed mods, or null when there are none. Carried on the transaction rather than passed
> down because the deepest hook site — an unimplemented effect op — is several frames below
> `reduce`. Null costs an optional-chain miss.


**L62–66**

> Randomness for effects evaluated deep inside an application, such as an `onDepleted` that heals
> `1d4` or a condition whose `defaultDuration` is rolled. Derived from the state's own generator,
> so a replay reproduces them exactly.


**L78–82**

> Mods watching for this specific event type. The `match` on the declaration is mandatory for
> this hook, so the gate below is a `Set` miss for every event nobody asked about — this runs
> hundreds of times a turn. Re-entrancy is guarded.


**L97**

> Replace one entity.


**L107**

> Coerce a DSL value into an entity id, or null when it is not one.


**L137–143**

> Every resistance, immunity and vulnerability that applies to a creature: the statblock, the
> ancestry, and carried items.
>
> Sources compose multiplicatively, so armour that halves fire on top of an ancestry that already
> halves it leaves a quarter.


**L164**

> Conditions a creature shrugs off, from its statblock or its ancestry.


**L176–180**

> Damage multiplier for a type, from the target's resistances. One multiplier covers resistance
> (0.5), immunity (0) and vulnerability (2), and lets a module express healing from a damage type
> as -1.


**L192**

> `unless` is what makes "immune to slashing, except silvered" expressible.


**L203**

> Change a resource, clamped, emitting the right events including depletion.


**L218**

> Both bounds depend on gear and conditions, so they are computed rather than stored; see


**L219**

> `stats.ts`.


**L240**

> Concentration is tested on damage, so the check lives here rather than on a turn boundary.


**L254–261**

> What happens when a pool bottoms out.
>
> The module's own `onDepleted` runs first, and death is declared afterwards only if the character
> is still at the floor. That is what lets `[heal 1, applyCondition downed]` mean "dropped to 0 and
> stabilised" without the engine needing a concept of dying. A resource with no `onDepleted` is
> unaffected.


**L272**

> `onDepleted` may itself damage, heal or kill, so a re-entry mark guards against a module


**L273**

> whose effects push the same pool back down. Per-call scratch, never serialized.


**L289**

> Death is whatever the module attached to the vital resource running out, and only if the module


**L290**

> did not just pull the character back off the floor.


**L303–307**

> Resource maxima, cached per entity object. Maxima depend on gear and conditions so they are
> computed rather than stored; the cache is keyed on the entity object, so any change invalidates
> it.


**L318**

> Minima, cached the same way and for the same reason.


**L329**

> Apply a condition, honouring immunity and the declared stacking rule.


**L349**

> An effect that names no duration gets the condition's own `defaultDuration`.


**L371**

> refresh: reset the clock without adding a second instance.


**L380**

> `onApply` fires when the condition takes hold, so a second dose of a `refresh` condition re-


**L381**

> runs it and one that was `ignore`d does not.


**L393**

> A condition's own duration, rolled if the module wrote it as dice.


**L416**

> Add or remove carried items, merging stacks.


**L460**

> Standing with one faction bleeds into its allies and rivals.


**L471–474**

> Apply a list of effect ops. Ops are applied in order and each reads the state the previous one
> left, so content can rely on sequencing.


**L482**

> The dead take no further damage, so an area effect cannot kill a corpse repeatedly and


**L483**

> emit a death event each time.


**L501**

> Healing the dead does nothing; raising is a separate effect.


**L547**

> Learning a clue twice is silent, and the recorded minute stays the first one: content


**L548**

> teaches the same clue from several places on purpose.


**L561**

> Move a creature's stance toward the party. Enrolling it in a fight already under way is


**L562**

> `settle`'s job.


**L590**

> Whether the purse can go below zero is the module's call: clamped, a party that


**L591**

> overspends is refused the difference; allowed, a module can model a debt.


**L605**

> Teleporting to a named place is resolved by the caller, which knows about maps; here it


**L606**

> records the intent as a custom event.


**L616**

> Where the sound came from: the named source, or whoever acted.


**L633**

> Refuse an op the engine does not implement. The `never` makes it a compile error to add


**L634**

> an op and forget the case; the refusal covers a module built against a newer engine.


**L638**

> Ask the mods before refusing, so a mod can add a genuinely new effect op that module JSON


**L639**

> uses and the studio edits without an engine case for it.


### packages/engine/src/rules/casting.ts


**L1–10**

> Casting: slots, points, concentration, rituals, and components.
>
> Two things decide the shape here:
>
> - The engine owns no spell list. Which abilities are spells is decided by an ability carrying a
>   `spellLevel`; an ability without one is unaffected by everything in this file.
> - A slot is spent, not reserved. `Entity.slotsUsed` counts what is gone, indexed by level, so a
>   character who levels up gains slots without anything reconciling a stored maximum.


**L44–47**

> The module's casting rules. The schema's own type rather than a hand-written mirror, which would
> need an `as unknown as` cast and could drift.


**L52**

> The casting half of a character's class, when it has one.


**L62**

> Whether this ability is a spell at all. Everything here turns on that.


**L67–70**

> Slots this character has at each level, before any are spent. `slotTable` is keyed by caster
> level and `progression` scales it, so a half-caster reads the same table at half the rate.


**L81**

> The highest declared level at or below the caster's, so a table that only lists odd levels


**L82**

> still works.


**L101**

> Slots left at each level: what the table gives, less what has been spent.


**L106–109**

> The lowest slot that can carry this spell, or null when none can. A spell may be cast from a
> higher slot than its own, so the search runs upward.


**L116**

> `maxSpellLevel` caps the ruleset's own ceiling.


**L127**

> The save DC a caster imposes, from the module's own formula.


**L134**

> The bonus a caster adds to a spell attack roll.


**L150**

> The attribute the class casts with, so one formula covers every caster.


**L152**

> This scope is built here rather than by `buildScope`, so the proficiency curve has to be


**L153**

> supplied explicitly or a caster's save DC and attack bonus cannot grow with level.


**L161–164**

> Whether the caster can supply what the spell asks for. Blocking conditions are named by the
> module's own `prevents` lists, so the engine knows nothing about what silence or manacles are.


**L170**

> Which action a component is, per the module. A ruleset that names neither cannot have its


**L171**

> casting interrupted that way.


**L195**

> Spend the material component a spell consumes, if it says so.


**L198**

> Only a `focus` component is reusable; a plain material is used up.


**L203–206**

> Pay for a spell, in whatever currency the module casts in. Returns the slot the spell went into,
> so the caller knows how far it was upcast. A ritual pays nothing and takes time instead.


**L223**

> Points first when a module casts both ways: burn the renewable resource before the scarce one.


**L257–260**

> Take up concentration on a spell, dropping whatever was already held. Only one is tracked per
> creature, so `maxConcurrent` above one means the previous spell is not dropped.


**L272–275**

> A blow that might shake a caster's hold on a spell. Called from the damage path, since that is
> when concentration is tested. The difficulty is the module's formula.


**L320**

> Give back the slots a rest of this kind restores.


### packages/engine/src/rules/check.ts


**L1–9**

> Resolution: rolling against a number.
>
> Every uncertain outcome funnels through here — skill checks, saving throws, attack rolls, opposed
> contests — so advantage, criticals and modifiers behave identically wherever they appear.
>
> How to roll is not decided here. The dice, what counts as a critical, and how advantage is
> expressed all come from `rules.resolution`.


**L36–39**

> What kind of d20 test this is. Only `criticalScope` reads it, so a ruleset can let a natural 20
> mean something on a swung sword and nothing on a lockpick.


**L43**

> Added to the roll: attribute modifiers, skill ranks, situational bonuses.


**L45**

> The number to beat. Defaults to the module's `defaultDifficulty`.


**L47–51**

> Which way the dice lean, and why this may be a list. A roll can be pulled both ways at once, so
> callers hand over everything that applies and `check` reconciles it, rather than leaving that
> to each caller.


**L53**

> Defaults to `check`, the least privileged of the three.


**L57**

> One swing or many, always as many.


**L63–66**

> One swing from however many apply, per `rules.resolution.swingStacking`. Exported because it is a
> pure function of a list of enums, with no module state and no dice.


**L87**

> `cancel`: one of each is enough to leave nothing, however many there are.


**L96**

> Resolve a named difficulty (`hard`) or a raw number.


**L106–116**

> A difficulty the module wrote as a formula rather than as a number.
>
> `difficultyOf` answers what number `hard` is; this answers what number fourteen is, less what
> they think of you — the difference between a gate and a price.
>
> Every `difficulty` in the format takes an {@link Expr} and a bare integer is a valid one, so
> older content reads unchanged. A formula that fails to produce a finite number yields `undefined`
> rather than zero, so the caller falls back to the module's default difficulty rather than the
> check becoming automatic.


**L126**

> A plain number is the common case and needs no scope built for it, which keeps the hot paths —


**L127**

> every trap search, every locked door — free of a scope construction.


**L136–139**

> Roll against a difficulty. The natural roll, before modifiers, decides criticals, which is why it
> is recorded separately.


**L154**

> Advantage notation is `2d20kh1`, so the kept die is the natural roll.


**L159**

> A roll of a kind the ruleset does not let crit is decided on its total alone.


**L185**

> Whether a roll counts as a hit. A critical always hits; a fumble never does.


**L190–194**

> The bonus a character brings to a skill check: the governing attribute's modifier plus their rank
> in the skill. Every skill-named outcome comes through here, so equipment reaches all of them by
> reaching `skillRankOf`.


**L206**

> Roll a skill check for one entity.


**L218**

> Whatever the caller knows, plus whatever the creature is carrying. Every skill roll comes


**L219**

> through here, so traps, dialogue, gates, loot and discovery are covered at once.


**L224–229**

> An opposed contest: both sides roll and the higher total wins. Ties go to the defender.
>
> Both records are rolled against nothing and then restated against each other, so `against` is the
> number that mattered and `outcome` says who won.


**L236**

> Each side is asked separately: a contest between a poisoned grappler and a blinded one pulls


**L237**

> both ways, and neither swing is the other's.


**L257–260**

> A roll measured against the other side rather than against nothing. A critical or a fumble is
> judged on the natural die, exactly as `check` decides it.


**L272–276**

> A saving throw. Bonuses come from the governing attribute plus any `saveBonuses` on the
> creature's statblock, so a module can make a creature resilient to one kind of effect without
> touching its attributes.


**L297**

> A class trained in this save adds the module's proficiency bonus, per `saveProficiencies`.


**L300**

> A save that declares its own default wins over the global difficulty whenever the caller named


**L301**

> none.


**L311**

> The save's own `defaultDifficulty`, which the module may write as a formula.


**L331**

> How much a critical multiplies damage.

## `packages/engine/src/rules/combat`

_182 comments_


### packages/engine/src/rules/combat/ai.ts


**L1–9**

> Monster and NPC turns.
>
> Behaviour is declared, not coded: a creature's `behaviour` list is a set of priority-ordered
> rules and the engine picks the first whose conditions hold.
>
> What the engine supplies is the part that needs the grid — choosing a target, deciding whether to
> close the distance, and pathing there.


**L40–44**

> Choose which ability to use. Rules are considered in priority order and the first usable one
> wins; a creature whose rules all fail falls back to a basic attack, so a statblock with no
> `behaviour` still fights.


**L56**

> Built explicitly rather than with a conditional spread: a spread produces an optional property


**L57**

> typed `X | undefined`, which the shared `Scope` index signature rejects under the editor's


**L58**

> stricter settings.


**L74–81**

> Whether going there would take this creature off its own ground.
>
> Measured on the destination, not on the step, so a creature refuses the chase rather than walking
> to the end of its rope — otherwise it could be towed one tile at a time.
>
> A creature with no anchor, or a module with no opinion, is never held back.


**L87–90**

> Take one creature's turn: move into range if needed, then act. Anything cleverer belongs in
> `behaviour` rules rather than in engine heuristics.


**L100**

> Only what this creature could actually perceive, so it cannot fight something on the far side


**L101**

> of the map.


**L108**

> Something that cannot act this turn still uses up its turn.


**L118**

> The leash gates the chase and nothing else: a creature drawn too far from its ground breaks


**L119**

> off, but still swings at whatever is beside it.


**L136**

> Nothing to attack: close on whoever is nearest.


**L147**

> Nothing in sight, but something was noticed: walk over to find out.


**L151–154**

> Break off and walk back to where it started — the other half of a leash, so a creature that has
> given up does not stand at the end of its rope.


**L170–174**

> Go and look at whatever was noticed. Arriving with nothing to show for it drops the alert, so a
> creature does not pace back to the same empty tile forever; a cold trail is forgotten the same
> way, by `perceiveInto`.


**L183**

> Holding a lead and acting on one are different: a creature whose `investigates` list does not


**L184**

> name that sense has nothing to do here, and the return value says so, so it is free to do


**L185**

> whatever else it would.


**L190**

> A lead that would take it off its ground is declined, and the alert is dropped rather than


**L191**

> held, so it does not re-decide every turn.


**L202**

> Standing on it and still nothing there: the lead is spent.


**L211**

> Only when the trail is fresh this minute, so a creature already moving toward something does


**L212**

> not narrate casting about every step.


**L224–234**

> Creatures act on what they noticed even when nobody is fighting, since turns only exist inside
> combat.
>
> Paced by the clock rather than by actions: `tiles` is how far anything may come while that much
> time passes. Tying it to elapsed minutes stops a free action freezing the world, and makes a long
> rest genuinely risky.
>
> Every creature draws from its own stream, keyed by who it is and when, so what one creature does
> cannot shift the dice for anything else.


**L249**

> How long a creature keeps caring, from `perception.curiosityMinutes`.


**L266**

> A tile a minute, the same pace the party walks.


**L271**

> Nothing to chase: a creature with ground of its own walks about on it, and one that has


**L272**

> strayed off it heads back. Both leave a trail while they do.


**L277–282**

> Walk about, or walk home.
>
> A random step rather than a path: a creature ambling around its own patch has no destination, and
> asking the pathfinder would cost an A* per creature per turn. Going home does use the pathfinder.


**L297**

> Off its ground with nothing to chase: back it goes, whatever its roaming habits, so a dungeon


**L298**

> does not empty itself into whichever room the party fought in.


**L309**

> Rolled once for the whole tick rather than once per step: per step, a creature that failed its


**L310**

> first roll would stand still for the whole stretch, so a long rest and a single minute would


**L311**

> move it equally far.


**L325**

> Every open neighbour that keeps it on its ground. Gathered rather than sampled, so the choice


**L326**

> is a single draw against a stable list and two identical runs step identically.


**L346–349**

> Walk toward a place until within `range`, spending the movement budget. A place, not a creature:
> closing on an enemy and walking over to a noise are the same movement.


**L365**

> Why it is moving decides how fast. A pace of zero is a creature that does not travel for that


**L366**

> reason at all.


**L397**

> Leaving a threatened tile may provoke, which can kill the mover mid-move.


**L404**

> A creature leaves a trail exactly as the party does.


**L409**

> Charged at what it actually walked: `pace` shapes how far it was willing to go, not how cheap


**L410**

> the ground was.


**L414–417**

> Run every non-player turn until it is a party member's turn again. Called after the player ends
> their turn, so control returns only when there is something to decide.


**L419**

> Bounded so a bad statblock cannot spin forever.


**L428**

> Party members decide for themselves.


### packages/engine/src/rules/combat/attack.ts


**L1–14**

> Using an ability, and hitting with it.
>
> One path for everything a creature actively does: a sword swing, a spell, a shout, a healing
> touch. The differences are declared in the ability, so the engine has no separate notion of
> attack versus spell.
>
> Order of resolution:
>
>   1. can it be used at all — costs, cooldown, requirement, conditions
>   2. who does it reach — range, line of sight, area shape
>   3. per target: attack roll, or saving throw, or neither
>   4. effects, scaled for criticals and successful saves

> cast at its own level.
