> Which way this ability's own roll always leans.


**L111**

> Spell fields. Absent on an ordinary ability, which is how magic is opt-in.


**L127–130**

> Scale damage in a set of ops — used for criticals and half-damage saves. Rounded by
> `rules.resolution.damageRounding`; at the default `round`, seven halved is four.


**L143–147**

> Evaluate an ability's costs. Costs are DSL expressions, so a spell can cost more at higher level.
> Rolled once and reused for the affordability check and the payment, so a random cost cannot
> differ between the two.


**L160**

> The first cost the actor cannot pay, as a readable reason.


**L178–181**

> Use an ability. Refusals are ordinary play — out of range, not enough focus, stunned — and each
> returns a reason the player can read.


**L196**

> A condition that forbids this kind of action stops it before anything else.


**L211**

> Cooldowns count in rounds, so out of combat there is nothing to count and an ability is always


**L212**

> ready.


**L231**

> — casting ————————————————————————————————————————————————


**L232**

> Everything below is skipped for an ability with no `spellLevel`, so a module with no magic is


**L233**

> unaffected.


**L260**

> A ritual is cast slowly instead of expensively, per `castingTime`.


**L273**

> The cooldown starts when the ability is spent, not when it is attempted: a refusal must not put


**L274**

> it on the shelf.


**L284**

> Sorted by entity then ability, so two runs that spent the same abilities compare equal.


**L295**

> A `none`-targeting ability still does whatever it declares.


**L309**

> Resolve one ability against one target.


**L317**

> The slot the spell went into, so upcasting knows how far above its own.


**L329**

> — an attack roll, when the ability declares one ——————————


**L338**

> The weapon is chosen before the roll: which attribute the blow uses can depend on what is in


**L339**

> the hand, which is what finesse means.


**L344**

> A spell attack uses the caster's own `spellcasting.attackBonus`, when the module declares


**L345**

> one.


**L350**

> Both sides of the blow: what the attacker is carrying, and what the target's own conditions


**L351**

> do to anyone swinging at them.


**L371**

> The blow carries the wielded weapon's qualities, so a resistance's `unless` is asking about


**L372**

> the weapon rather than the ability. Damage the ability tagged itself is left alone, and if


**L373**

> the ability named no damage the weapon supplies it.


**L378**

> A critical multiplies damage by whatever the module says, then runs any extra `onCritical`


**L379**

> effects.


**L389**

> — a saving throw, when the ability declares one ——————————


**L391**

> The spell's own DC when the ability names one, then the caster's formula, then the module's


**L392**

> ordinary difficulty.


**L427**

> — no roll: it simply happens ———————————————————————————


**L442–445**

> The weapon a character is actually wielding. Weapon-kind items only: a shield and a ring are

**L456–460**

> Which attribute this blow is swung with. The ability names one; the weapon's properties may offer

> swing and an agility one.

### packages/engine/src/rules/combat/targeting.ts


**L1–9**

> Targeting: who can be hit, from where.
>
> Range, reach, line of sight, cover and area shapes are real geometry rather than tags, so a wall
> genuinely protects you and a fireball genuinely catches your own party.
>
> Distances are in tiles. The module measures range in its own units, so conversion happens once,
> here, using the tile size derived from `rules.sizes`.


**L22**

> Fallback tile size when a module declares no sizes.


**L25–28**

> How many module units one tile represents. Taken from the smallest declared size's `space`, since
> that is the creature that fits in exactly one tile.


**L35**

> Convert a module distance into tiles, rounding down.


**L41**

> A creature's reach in tiles: its size's natural reach, at least one.


**L47**

> A creature that declares no size falls back to `rules.defaultSize`.


**L57**

> Movement allowance in tiles for one turn.


**L70**

> Creeping is slower than walking, and running is not free either.


**L74**

> A creature with no declared movement still shuffles one tile; being unable to move at all


**L75**

> should come from a condition, not from missing data.


**L85**

> Living entities on the same map as the actor.


**L90**

> Whether two entities are on opposing sides.


**L99**

> Distance in tiles.


**L101**

> Cover the target benefits from, if any.


**L105–109**

> Whether a target can be reached from a position, and what protects it. Cover is read from the
> terrain between the two, and the module decides how much by declaring the cover type on the
> terrain.


**L123**

> Cover comes from the tile immediately adjacent to the target along the line of fire — the thing


**L124**

> it is standing behind.


**L129**

> Only terrain between shooter and target counts as cover.


**L142**

> Cover so complete there is no shot at all, per `blocksTargeting` on the cover type.


**L148**

> Defence bonus granted by a cover type.


**L165–168**

> Everyone an ability would affect. Area abilities catch whoever is standing in the shape,
> including the caster's own party; `affects` is how content narrows that.


**L179**

> An area ability is resolved by its shape rather than by the target list.


**L225**

> Even a sweeping ability only reaches what it can see and get to.


**L252**

> The nearest hostile an actor can see, for AI target selection.


**L261**

> Uncapped unless a caller says otherwise: how far a creature can notice something is a question


**L262**

> about that creature, so the limit belongs to whoever is asking.


**L274**

> Ties break on entity id so AI target choice stays reproducible.


### packages/engine/src/rules/combat/turn.ts


**L1–9**

> Turn structure: initiative, the action economy, and reactions.
>
> What a turn is comes from the module. `rules.actionTypes` declares the budget and abilities name
> which they consume; the engine only tracks what has been spent.
>
> Combat begins when something hostile can perceive the party and ends when one side is gone. It is
> not a mode the player enters deliberately.


**L69**

> Roll initiative for everyone present and sort them.


**L83**

> Ties break on entity id, so two identical runs order the round the same way.


**L88**

> A fresh action budget for whoever is about to act.


**L93–96**

> Put the player behind the character whose turn it is. Commands act as the selected character, so
> without this the last-selected member spends every party turn.


**L103–107**

> Begin combat, if anything hostile can perceive the party. Sharing a map is not enough: enrolling
> every creature in a dungeon the moment the party steps inside would have monsters take turns
> against someone they have never seen. Only those who noticed take part.


**L119**

> At the `aggro` threshold: merely catching a scent is not enough to start a fight.


**L151–163**

> Enrol anyone who has turned hostile since the fight started.
>
> `maybeStartCombat` returns immediately once combat is running, so a creature whose disposition
> changes mid-fight would never appear in the initiative order.
>
> Newcomers are appended rather than sorted into place: `combat.turn` is an index into `order`, so
> appending cannot change whose turn it is. They act at the end of this round and roll in with
> everyone else at the start of the next.
>
> The awareness boundary applies here as at the start: turning hostile does not enrol something
> that has not noticed anybody.

## `packages/engine/src/rules`

_150 comments_


### packages/engine/src/rules/conditions.ts


**L1–10**

> Condition lifecycle.
>
> Everything that expires runs through one tick, so durations cannot leak: a condition applied by a
> spell, a trap, or a terrain tile is the same object and ages the same way.
>
> Order within a tick is fixed: `onTick` effects fire first, then durations decrease, then anything
> at zero expires and fires `onExpire`. A poison that deals damage on the round it wears off is
> correct — it was still active for that round.


**L22**

> The four ways a condition can lean a roll.


**L39**

> Which timings a save-ends pass covers.


**L48–52**

> Give an entity its saves against the conditions that allow one. "Save ends" is a save, a
> difficulty and a timing; without this pass a condition with a save behaves exactly like one
> without, and the `saved` reason on `conditionRemoved` is unreachable.


**L88**

> A condition's save difficulty, which the module may write as a formula.


**L104**

> Whether a condition forbids an action type, e.g. `stunned` blocking actions.


**L113–122**

> Every swing an entity's conditions impose on one kind of roll.
>
> A list rather than a single answer, because reconciling them is `resolveSwing`'s job and
> `check`'s alone to do — collecting here and deciding there is what stops two call sites
> disagreeing.
>
> Takes a module rather than a transaction, unlike `preventsAction`, because the roll sites that
> need this do not all have one.


**L137–140**

> Advance every condition on one entity by a round. `rng` is passed in rather than drawn from state
> because a tick happens inside a turn that already has its own sub-stream.


**L145**

> 1. Effects fire while the condition is still active.


**L160**

> 2. Age them, and collect what has run out.


**L169**

> Null duration means "until removed" — it never ages out.


**L178**

> Always write the aged conditions back: returning early when nothing expired would discard the


**L179**

> decremented durations, and conditions would tick forever without running out.


**L183**

> 3. Expiry effects run once the condition is already gone, so an `onExpire` that reapplies it


**L184**

>    does not immediately expire again.


**L198**

> Tick every entity on the current map.


### packages/engine/src/rules/config.ts


**L1–17**

> Ruleset knobs, read once.
>
> The engine reaches `module.source.rules.*` from a hundred places. These accessors are the half of
> those numbers that used to be literals in code.
>
> Two rules hold here:
>
> - No `??` chains. `compileModule` runs the document through Zod, so a declared default is already
>   present; restating one would be a second source of truth.
> - Resolved, not merely read, where the module's answer takes more than a property access — a
>   default that falls back to the first declared entry, or a rounding mode that has to become a
>   function.
>
> Resolution is memoized per module because these are read per operation and a module never
> changes.


**L21**

> How a scaled number is turned back into a whole one.


**L32**

> What a successful save against `half` leaves.


**L34**

> The floor a passive score is measured from.


**L36**

> Whether a resisting side rolls, or is measured standing still.


**L38**

> Applied to damage after resistance, criticals, and saves.


**L40**

> Applied to a reputation spill across faction relations.


**L42**

> Whether the purse may go below zero.


**L44**

> Minutes in an hour, for the calendar.


**L46–49**

> How a creature gets about when it declares nothing: the module's own default, then the first
> mode it declares at all, and null for a ruleset with no movement modes.


**L76**

> What a successful saving throw against `onSuccess: "half"` leaves.


**L81–85**

> How a scaled damage number is rounded. Every path that scales damage goes through this —
> resistance, a critical, and a save for half — because a second rounding rule hidden in one of
> them is a systematic difference.


**L90**

> The baseline a passive score is measured from.


**L95–98**

> The movement mode a creature uses when it declares none. The same shape as `defaultStanceOf` and
> `reachOf`'s size fallback: declared first, then the first entry, then nothing.


### packages/engine/src/rules/implied.ts


**L1–11**

> Conditions that hold without having been applied.
>
> `implies` describes a condition that supersedes another: being unconscious
> is being prone, without anyone having to remember to apply prone as well.
>
> This lives on its own rather than in `conditions.ts` because both the
> condition lifecycle and the stat pipeline need it, and `conditions.ts`
> already depends on `check.ts`, which depends on `stats.ts`. A shared leaf
> with no imports of its own is the version of that with no cycle in it.


**L20–26**

> Conditions implied by the ones an entity already has, transitively.
>
> Never one it already holds directly. `unconscious implies prone` on a
> creature that is also prone in its own right must not count prone twice, or
> a condition would be worth more for having arrived by two routes.


**L44–54**

> Every condition that counts right now: the ones applied, and the ones those
> imply.
>
> An implied condition has no lifecycle of its own. It does not tick, it does
> not expire, and it cannot be saved against or removed on its own -- it holds
> for exactly as long as the condition implying it does. That is what
> `implies` has always meant by superseding, and it is why this is a derived
> view rather than entries written into `entity.conditions`: writing them
> there would mean removal had to know why each one arrived.


### packages/engine/src/rules/passives.ts


**L1–12**

> Passive rules: what an ancestry is and what a carried thing does.
>
> `ancestries[].traits` and `items[].procs` are the same shape — a `RuleSchema`
> of `{ when?, then }` — and both were accepted, validated, and run by nothing.
> A dwarf's stoutness and a cloak's ward were flavour text with a JSON body.
>
> They are evaluated rather than applied-once because they are *passive*: the
> condition is re-asked, so "while bloodied" means while bloodied. A rule that
> should fire once says so by setting a flag in its own `when`, exactly as a
> trigger does.


**L34**

> Every passive rule that currently applies to one creature.


**L43**

> Carried, not merely equipped: a proc on a charm in your pack is still on


**L44**

> you. An item that should only work in hand says so with its own `when`.


**L52–57**

> Run the passive rules on one creature.
>
> A rule with no `when` fires every time it is asked, which is what a module
> means by an unconditional passive — and why most of them carry one.


**L62**

> Mods run alongside the module's own passives, and are consulted even when


**L63**

> an entity has none of its own — a mod granting a trait to everything would


**L64**

> otherwise be invisible on entities the module never gave traits to.


**L83**

> A malformed condition hides the rule rather than stopping the turn —


**L84**

> the same courtesy quest availability already extends.


**L93**

> Run them for the whole party, which is where they are asked from.

### packages/engine/src/save.ts


**L1–15**

> Saving and loading.
>
> A save is the `GameState` object and nothing else — no side tables, no derived caches. That is
> why maxima, Guard and initiative are computed rather than stored: a cached number would be a
> second source of truth a save could disagree with.
>
> Two guards:
>
>   - Module identity. A save records the module id, version and content hash it was made against,
>     and loading it into an edited module is refused rather than producing a character whose class
>     no longer exists.
>   - Schema version. Migrations run in order, so an old save is brought forward step by step
>     rather than needing a migration per version pair.

> One module build this save has been written under.


**L25**

> The `savedAt` of the save that first wrote this hash.

> it can answer which change broke a save. Optional, so a save written before lineage existed
> still loads.

> Mods active when this was written, as `<id>-<hash>`, sorted.

## `packages/engine/src/sim`

_496 comments_


### packages/engine/src/sim/agenda.ts


**L1–10**

> The world's own clock.
>
> Everything here happens whether or not the party is watching: rumours travel, memories fade,
> factions cool off, creatures learn, and quests time out.
>
> Determinism is the constraint. A day's off-screen activity draws from `world:day:<n>`, so it is a
> pure function of the seed and the day number, independent of what the party spent their turns
> doing.


**L21–25**

> What a creature kind has learned about the party. Held in flags rather than a dedicated table so
> it serializes with everything else and content can read it: a statblock's `behaviour` rule can
> check `flags.learned:bog_hound:encounters`.


**L30–33**

> The module's learning settings. A read, not a merge — see the note on `witnessConfig` in
> `deeds.ts`.


**L38–41**

### packages/engine/src/sim/arcs.ts


**L1–7**

> Story arcs: the shape a run has, above its quests.
>
> An arc's status is derived from its quests, never stored. Storing it would create a second source
> of truth that a save could disagree with — the same argument `stageIndexOf` makes for which stage
> a quest is on.

> Where every arc stands. `failed` beats `complete` beats `active`: an arc with a failed quest in
> it has gone wrong however much of the rest was finished, which is what lets a module write a
> story that can be lost.

**L50**

**L80**

**L85–93**

> The engine's record that an ending has already been reached and announced.
>
> A flag rather than a field on `GameState`, in the same family as `gate:<id>:open` — an existing
> save reads it as absent, so there is nothing to migrate.
>
> Needed because `start.postVictory: 'continue'` leaves `outcome` at `playing`, and a finished arc
> stays finished: without a record of its own the ending would be reached again on every reduction.


**L98–102**

> Arc status as the DSL sees it. Populated for every declared arc rather than only the started
> ones, so `arcs` is a closed namespace and a misspelled arc id is a loud error rather than a
> silent `unstarted`.


### packages/engine/src/sim/clock.ts


**L1–12**

> The world clock, beyond counting minutes.
>
> `world.time` declares day phases, a month length and month names, and none of
> them were read — so a module could describe dawn, dusk and the month of
> Firstmelt and the game would tell you it was "day 12 07:15".
>
> Everything here is **derived from `state.minute`**, never stored and never
> ticked. That is the same rule scent trails follow, and for the same reason: a
> value that is recomputed cannot drift out of step with the clock it describes,
> and a save cannot disagree with itself.


**L23**

> Days since the game began, counting the first as 1.


**L26**

> 1-based, so it reads like a date rather than an array index.


**L34–40**

> Which phase of the day it is, or null underground.
>
> A barrow has no dusk. `layer: 'underworld'` is what a module uses to say so,
> and this is the one place that distinction can mean anything mechanical —
> otherwise "overworld" and "underworld" are two words for the same thing.


**L54**

> The last phase that has already started. Before the first one begins, the


**L55**

> day is still in the previous day's last phase — midnight is night, not


**L56**

> nothing, and wrapping is the only reading that makes the cycle closed.


**L64**

> The calendar date, from a module's own month length and names.


**L89**

> Which layer the party is standing on, for the phase.


**L100**

> A biome may say so on the area's behalf — an area that never declared a


**L101**

> layer inherits the one its biome describes.

### packages/engine/src/sim/gates.ts


**L1–10**

> Gates: barriers and how they open.
>
> A locked door, a warded seal, a riddle and a toll are the same shape — a requirement that opens
> it, an optional roll to force it, and effects either way — which is why the world can be gated by
> a key, a spell, a faction rank or a completed quest without any of them being special.
>
> When a gate refuses, the party is told what is missing: "the brass key, or a steady hand with
> picks" is a lead where "it is locked" is a dead end.

> Plain-language description of what a requirement is asking for.

**L52**

> the engine naming a thing, and that is keyed.

> asked to pick the lock.

> Already standing open. Returning here must not re-run `onOpen`, re-consume the key, or re-roll


**L99**

> the lockpick.

**L129**

**L154**

> Every knob comes from `narrative.memory`. `mode` decides who is driving: `simulated`, `manual`,

### packages/engine/src/sim/lore.ts


**L1–14**

> What the party has found out.
>
> A quest records what somebody told you to do; lore records what you worked out.
>
> Three properties decide the shape:
>
>   - Learning is permanent and unordered, so state is a flat id → minute map and everything else
>     here is derived.
>   - A thread declares its whole set, which is what lets the journal say three of five rather than
>     listing three facts and implying nothing.
>   - An unknown entry has no text. `loreByThread` returns the slot and withholds the words, so a
>     front end cannot accidentally render a clue the party has not earned.

> World minute it was learned, or null while unknown.

**L99–103**

> Known entries belonging to no thread, newest first. Loose lore is the module's business rather

> Thread progress as the DSL sees it. Populated for every declared thread rather than only the

### packages/engine/src/sim/memoryscope.ts


**L1–17**

> What is remembered, as something the DSL can read.
>
> `requirement.memories` compiles to `{exists: "memory.<who>.<deedKind>"}`, so a gate on what
> somebody remembers is only as good as the `memory` namespace in scope.
>
> Two things make this cheap enough to put in `buildScope`, which runs several times per entity per
> turn:
>
>   * The five `who` keys are getters, memoized on first read, so a scope built for something that
>     never mentions memory costs one object literal.
>   * `memory` is a plain object whose keys are getters, not a getter called `memory`. Eight call
>     sites do `{ ...buildScope(...) }`; a shallow spread copies the reference and the inner
>     getters survive it.
>
> `lookup()` in the DSL walks the scope with plain property access, so a getter is invisible to it.

> What the DSL sees for one remembered deed kind.

> When it happened, as a day index — the same units as `world.day`, so `requirement.withinDays`

> 0..1, decaying on the module's forgetting curve.


**L35**

> How many retellings away from having seen it. 0 means they were there.


**L41**

> The strongest record each holder has of every deed kind they know.


**L64**

> Memoize a zero-argument builder behind a property.


**L76–80**

> The `memory` namespace for a scope. `subject` is whose recollection `speaker` means: the person
> being talked to in a conversation, the reactor in a reaction, and otherwise the actor the scope
> was built for.


**L91**

> What this person knows. `self` is the same thing under a name that reads properly outside a


**L92**

> conversation.


**L96**

> What the party did, rather than what it knows: witnesses never include characters (`deeds.ts`


**L97**

> skips them), so "the party saw it" is not something the simulation records.


**L115**

> Anybody at all knows it. Distinct from the speaker, or "word has got around" would be


**L116**

> indistinguishable from "this one person saw you".


**L125**

> What the subject's own people know.


### packages/engine/src/sim/quests.ts


**L1–10**

> Quests: stages, objectives, and completion.
>
> Objectives watch the event stream rather than being polled: when a creature dies, a place is
> reached or a flag is set, every active objective gets a chance to notice. Quest logic therefore
> stays out of the actions themselves.
>
> Common shapes (`kill`, `collect`, `reach`, `talk`) are recognised directly; `custom` takes a DSL
> predicate for everything else.


**L74**

> Every objective in a quest, flattened across stages in order.


**L84–88**

> Which stage a quest is on, derived rather than stored: the first stage still holding an
> unfinished required objective. Deriving it means no new state field and no save migration, and it
> cannot drift out of step with the completed list. Stage-less objectives never move the cursor.


**L99**

> Run a stage's or quest's effects against the party leader.


**L108**

> Start a quest, if it is not already running and its gate allows.


**L141**

> Opening the quest opens its first stage, so a stage's `onStart` setup runs.


**L148–152**

> Start every quest the module marks as beginning on its own. Run as the party is placed, inside
> the arrival transaction, so `onStart` can run and its event reaches the transcript; `newGame` has
> no transaction.


**L161–164**

> Note quests the world has opened up but nobody has offered yet. An `available` quest is one the
> party could take if they knew to ask, which is what the journal shows under "available".


**L177**

> A malformed predicate hides the quest; it must not stop the turn.


**L192–195**

> Give up on a quest. It drops back to available rather than vanishing, and progress is cleared:
> walking away and coming back means doing the work.


**L221–224**

> Whether an event satisfies an objective. The recognised shapes cover what quests usually ask for;
> anything else declares a `when` predicate and is checked against world state.


**L242**

> Arriving at a place with no interior map of its own, which emits no `enteredMap`, so a


**L243**

> "reach the mill" objective can still complete.


**L260**

> How many times an objective has been satisfied so far, for counted goals.


**L265–268**

> Offer events to every active quest. Called once after an action resolves, with everything that
> action produced.


**L278**

> A quest can fail out from under the party — the giver dies, time runs out.


**L301**

> Ordered quests only accept progress on the next unfinished objective.


**L307**

> An objective can declare what it takes to be active. Not threaded into `stageIndexOf`: on


**L308**

> an ordered quest the cursor sits on the gated objective and the quest waits, which keeps


**L309**

> the stage cursor derived from `completedObjectives` alone.


**L322**

> On an event-driven kind, `when` is an extra gate the event must also satisfy, not a


**L323**

> second way to finish — that would give a `kill` objective two completion paths.


**L376**

> Finishing the last required objective of a stage closes it and opens the next. The cursor is


**L377**

> derived, so this is the only place that has to notice.


**L418**

> — rewards ——————————————————————————————————————————————


**L430**

> A completed quest can open the next one in a chain.


**L443**

> What the world will remember about this.


**L466**

> Evaluate a reward expression, which may be a plain number or DSL.


**L478–481**

> Award experience to the whole party, and level anyone who has earned it. Each crossed level is
> applied in order, because a module may grant something at 2 that something at 3 depends on.


**L482–490**

> Experience for what the party killed, from `content.monsters[].xp`.
>
> There is no switch for this: a ruleset that does not want experience for killing gives its
> creatures none, which is the schema default.
>
> Read from the statblock rather than from the corpse. `Entity.xp` holds both what a character has
> earned and what a monster is worth, so the authored number is the one to trust.


**L495**

> Only what the party brought down. A creature killed by another creature, or by the ground it


**L496**

> was standing on, teaches the party nothing.


**L543**

> Everything reaching one level does, beyond the number itself.


**L551**

> What the class has unlocked by now. A union rather than a replacement, so an ability granted by


**L552**

> an ancestry or an item is not revoked.


**L565**

> Then the module's own effects for reaching it, scoped to the character who levelled rather than


**L566**

> the party leader — `applyEffects` uses the leader, which would give everyone's level-up bonus


**L567**

> to one person.


**L577–581**

> Quests this person can hand over, that the party could take right now. Answers both
> `npcs[].offersQuests` and `quests[].giver`, so a front end can offer the job and a bare `talk`
> can mention it without a dialogue tree.


**L594**

> Sorted, so two runs offer the same jobs in the same order.


**L600**

> Already taken, already done — unless it is one you can take again.


### packages/engine/src/sim/questview.ts


**L1–12**

> What the player is allowed to read about their quests.
>
> The journal and the objective line on screen are the same question asked at
> two lengths, so they are answered in one place. A front end that formatted its
> own view of quest state would eventually disagree with the journal about what
> the party is doing, and the player would believe whichever was on screen.
>
> Nothing here is stored. Progress counts come from the flags the reducer
> already writes, and the stage cursor is derived, so this cannot fall out of
> step with the state it describes.


**L25**

> How many of `count` are in hand.


**L28**

> Which stage it belongs to, or null for a quest-level objective.


**L37**

> 1-based for display; 0 when the quest declares no stages.


**L46**

> The party's whole quest log, active first.


**L72**

> A hidden objective stays hidden until it is met — that is the point of


**L73**

> it. Stages the party has not reached are not spoilers to be listed.


**L105**

> An objective with no authored text still has to say something, so its id


**L106**

> reads as a phrase rather than as an identifier.


**L122–127**

> The one line worth keeping on screen: what to do next.
>
> The oldest active quest wins, because that is the thread the party picked up
> first and the one they are most likely to mean.


**L152–159**

> The journal, grouped by the arcs a module declares.
>
> `narrative.arcs` existed and nothing read it, so a module that described its
> story in three acts showed the player a flat list of jobs. Quests belonging to
> no arc come last, under no heading — which is what a module with no arcs at
> all sees, unchanged.


### packages/engine/src/sim/reactions.ts


**L1–16**

> Turning events into reactions.
>
> `content.monsters[].reactions` and `content.npcs[].reactions` declare twelve triggers —
> `seePlayer`, `allyHurt`, `allyKilled`, `selfHurt`, `lowHealth`, `combatStart`, `combatEnd`,
> `turnStart`, `witnessDeed`, `questComplete`, `itemShown`, `custom` — and all of them are
> broadcast from here.
>
> The dispatch is event-driven rather than hooked at each origin. Damage alone arrives from six
> places — a player ability, an AI ability, a trap, terrain, a condition tick, and a resource
> bottoming out — so `damaged` is one event, emitted once, from all of them.
>
> It cannot be driven from inside `adjustResource`: reaction effects call `applyOps`, which calls
> `adjustResource`, and nothing but a scratch set meant for something else would stand between that
> and unbounded recursion.


**L25**

> Everyone present who might react, the subject excepted.


**L31–34**

> Fan a batch of events out to whoever declared a reaction to them. `died` is deliberately absent:
> `allyKilled` is broadcast from `performAbility`, where it has always been.


**L70**

> "Ally" is read the way `isHostileTo` reads every other question about sides: anyone who


**L71**

> is not hostile to the victim. That makes a monster pack care about its own without a


**L72**

> module declaring a faction.


**L81**

> Exactly the people who saw it, which is the point of the witness roll in `recordDeed`.


**L82**

> The event carries the deed's id rather than its doer, so who to be annoyed at comes back


**L83**

> out of the log.


**L101**

> `deed` and `startQuest` are the reducer's own vocabulary and are handled there; re-


**L102**

> broadcasting them here would fire a reaction on the machinery rather than on the event


**L103**

> the content meant.


**L120–125**

> Reactions to being noticed. Split from the event fan-out because noticing is a difference between
> two perception passes rather than an event. Passing the pairs in rather than importing
> `runReactions` into `sim/senses.ts` avoids a cycle: `rules/combat/turn.ts` already imports
> `canPerceive` from there.


### packages/engine/src/sim/senses.ts


**L1–11**

> Perception: whether a creature can perceive something, asked in one place so combat entry, target
> selection, witnessing and room description all agree.
>
> Everything reduces to a single number: signal strength at the observer, between zero and one.
> Thresholds rather than ranges decide what that number means, which lets a cold trail be weak at
> close range.
>
> The engine names no sense. Sight, hearing and smell are whatever the module calls them; a module
> that declares none gets one nameless implicit sense.


**L25**

> How a signal gets from there to here.


**L28**

> What stops it on the way.


**L31**

> How it weakens with distance.


**L34**

> How much signal is enough to do what.


**L37–40**

> A sense with every default resolved and every distance already in tiles. Built once per module
> and cached, like `TerrainIndex`, because it is read on every perception query.


**L46**

> Baseline reach in tiles, converted from module units exactly once.


**L48**

> Conditions this sense works through anyway, e.g. blindsight and blinded.


**L50**

> Minutes a trace stays perceptible. Zero means this sense leaves none.


**L52**

> Tiles a lingering trace spreads outward per minute as it thins.


**L54**

> Signal kept once it has spread: the same scent over more ground.


**L56**

> Minutes a percept stays remembered. Zero means it is forgotten at once.


**L58**

> What noticing something this way reads like, strong and faint.


**L61**

> What using this sense deliberately reads like when it finds nothing.


**L63**

> Signal needed to notice, to go and look, and to fight. Ordered.


**L69–71**

> Structurally what `TargetingContext` is, declared here so perception does not depend on combat.


**L78–86**

> The sense a module gets when it declares none.
>
> `defaultRange` is the schema's default of 60 module units, which at the fallback tile size of 5
> is twelve tiles. A cliff falloff yields one inside that reach with a clear line and zero
> otherwise, so `strength > 0` is exactly `hasLineOfSight(...) && distance(...) <= 12`.
>
> The id is empty rather than "sight": the engine names no sense.


**L110**

> A sense exactly as the module wrote it, before defaults are resolved.


**L130**

> Every sense the module declares, in declaration order — or the implicit one.


**L143**

> Converted from module units to tiles here, so nothing downstream has to track which unit


**L144**

> it holds.


**L163**

> One sense by id, or undefined.


**L168–171**

> How far this creature reaches with this sense, in tiles. Resolves like `speedOf`: the creature's
> own declaration first, the rule's default behind it.


**L179**

> The creature's own declaration first, the sense's default behind it. A monster's statblock wins


**L180**

> over its ancestry.


**L190**

> A condition can shut the sense off unless the sense ignores it. Checked here because every use


**L191**

> of a sense's reach comes through this function.


**L202–205**

> Whether anything the creature is under closes this sense. Read as a pair: a condition names the
> senses it shuts off, and a sense names the conditions it works through anyway.


**L219–222**

> How strongly a signal from `from` reaches `observer`. The one place propagation, barriers and
> falloff are applied; everything else here composes it. Distance is measured the way movement is.


**L239**

> How far the signal had to travel. A `line` sense measures the way movement does; a `field`


**L240**

> sense floods, which is why a smell reaches around a corner and a shout does not.


**L252**

> Measured on the observer's own map, so a creature cannot perceive through the geometry of


**L253**

> somewhere it is not standing.


**L259**

> Whether the signal has arrived yet. What reaches you is where something was, however long the


**L260**

> air took to bring it.


**L264**

> Thinner for having spread out. Eased from full at the source to `spreadRetention` at the edge


**L265**

> of reach, rather than switched on at the first tile.


**L272**

> Whether this sense arrives at once, or has to make its way over.


**L277–285**

> Has the signal had time to get here?
>
> A sense that does not travel is always yes. For one that does, the front is `spreadPerMinute`
> tiles out per minute since it was given off.
>
> An unknown emission time is treated as long past rather than as just now: the callers that omit
> it are asking about a place rather than a creature.


**L291**

> Whether a straight signal survives the trip.


**L303**

> `impassable` blocks sound; terrain that hides you from sight may still carry every noise you


**L304**

> make.


**L313–316**

> Whether a tile is solid enough to stop a signal. Uses the terrain's own `passable` flag, not
> `isPassable`, which asks whether a particular creature could walk there.


**L321**

> The tiles a straight signal crosses, endpoints included.


**L336–345**

> How far a spreading signal has to go to reach each tile.
>
> A uniform-cost flood from the observer, not the pathfinder: `reachable()` weights by movement
> cost and honours movement modes, which would make deep water smell distant and stop a hound
> smelling across a stream it cannot swim.
>
> Flooding from the observer rather than from each trace keeps it to one flood per creature per
> question.


**L389–392**

> Cached per state object, which is safe because state is replaced rather than mutated: a new state
> is a new cache.


**L395**

> A stance as the module wrote it.


**L405**

> The stance a creature is holding, or none.


**L411–414**

> How loud, how rank, how visible a creature is right now. Stance sets it and a declared skill
> quietens it further. No dice: the same approach always sounds the same. One is ordinary presence.


**L430**

> Never silent outright by default; whether perfect stealth is possible is the module's to


**L431**

> decide.


**L435–439**

> Whether `observer` perceives `subject` well enough to act on it. Replaces `hasLineOfSight(...) &&
> distance(...) <= 12` at every call site. Asked of every declared sense, so a creature that cannot
> see you may still hear you.


**L455–458**

> The same question about a place rather than a creature. A deed happens on a tile and a witness
> perceives the act, not the actor.


**L469**

> When the signal started coming from there. Absent means long enough ago.


**L500**

> The furthest this creature could notice anything, in tiles.


**L509–512**

> The stance a creature uses when it has chosen none: the module's declared default, then the first
> stance it declares, then null for a module with no stances.


**L519–523**

> How strong a trace still is, from how long ago it was left. Always derived, never stored, so
> nothing has to tick. The zero point is an integer comparison on minutes, so "faded" and "pruned"
> cannot disagree.


**L534–537**

> How far a trace has got from where it was left, in tiles. This is the front of the cloud, not a
> bonus to the observer's reach, and it decides whether the smell has arrived at all.


**L543**

> Whether any declared sense leaves traces at all.


**L548–552**

> Leave traces on a tile a creature just entered. Faded traces on the same tile are dropped in the
> same write, so repeatedly walked ground never accumulates. A ruleset whose senses leave nothing
> writes nothing.


**L569**

> Prune this tile while it is already being rewritten.


**L573**

> A creature crossing its own trail refreshes it rather than layering.


**L580**

> What the ground will hold. A fact about the terrain rather than the creature, so it scales


**L581**

> the emission rather than the sense.


**L593–604**

> Keep only the freshest few traces per sense on one tile.
>
> Trimmed to a few rather than to one: two creatures passing in opposite directions is genuinely
> two things.
>
> Matters for speed more than space: {@link perceive} reads every trace on every tile, for every
> sense, for every creature, every turn.
>
> The result carries a total order — oldest first, ties broken on sense then on who left it —
> because truncation on a partial order would drop different traces on two equivalent runs.


**L618**

> Sorted oldest first, so the tail is what is still worth smelling.


**L625**

> Oldest first, and total: the order a tile's traces are stored in.


**L633–636**

> The one place a trace is constructed. Field order in an object literal is serialization order,
> and saved state is compared by its serialization, so equal traces must be written the same way.


**L641**

> Write one tile's traces, dropping the key entirely when none are left.


**L658–661**

> Drop faded traces everywhere. Housekeeping rather than decay: it only stops a save growing, and
> catches the corridor walked once and never returned to.


**L694–700**

> The tiles a creature perceives with one sense — what to draw and what to describe.
>
> Not the same operation as {@link canPerceive}: this is a field, computed by shadowcasting for a
> straight sense and by flooding for a spreading one, and shadowcasting uses a round disk where
> perception measures the way movement does.


**L724–727**

> The sense a module draws its map with. Falls back to the first declared sense, then to the
> implicit one, so something is always drawable.


**L734**

> One thing perceived, whether it is standing there or merely passed through.


**L740**

> True when the source is there now, false when it is a trace it left.


**L744–747**

> Everything a creature perceives right now, strongest first. Living creatures and cold trails come
> back in the same shape, so following a scent and watching a target are one code path.


**L758**

> Creatures, where they actually are.


**L761**

> What it registers. Enemies only by default; a module widens it per creature, which is how a


**L762**

> wolf tracks a deer and a shopkeeper sees anyone.


**L765**

> Measured from when it arrived, not from now: something that has just stepped into the room


**L766**

> has not yet filled it.


**L776**

> And the traces they left behind, which is what makes a trail followable.


**L778**

> A creature that does not read traces. It can still perceive the source directly.


**L794**

> The trace is its own clock: it began spreading where it was laid, so an old one has


**L795**

> reached further than a fresh one.


**L808**

> Whether a trace is worth following: left by something this creature registers.


**L815**

> A trace outlives whoever left it, so a trail of something long gone is still followable.


**L820**

> Strongest first; ties settled by sense order then subject, so the order is total.


**L832**

> The one place an alert is constructed — same reason as {@link newMark}.


**L843–846**

> Fold what a creature perceives into what it remembers. At most one alert per sense and subject.
> Writes nothing when nothing changed, so a quiet turn costs no state.


**L868**

> A fresher or stronger reading replaces the older one: the alert is the best current idea of


**L869**

> where the thing is, not a history of glimpses.


**L893–897**

> Somebody who has just become aware of a party member. Returned rather than acted on: importing
> `runReactions` here would close a cycle, since `rules/combat/turn.ts` already imports
> `canPerceive`.


**L903–906**

> Let everything on the party's map notice what there is to notice. Returns the pairs that are new
> this pass: noticing is a difference between two perception passes, not an emitted event.


**L924**

> Somebody who has just perceived a party member. Deduped on who was noticed rather than on


**L925**

> which sense noticed them, so a creature that both sees and smells you reacts once.


**L938**

> Announce what the party just noticed. Creatures act on it silently; the player has only the


**L939**

> prose.


**L962**

> The strongest thing a creature would act on at a given threshold, or null.


**L975**

> Which sense it trusts. Absent means whichever is strongest, since `alerts` is already strongest


**L976**

> first.


**L980**

> Named senses are a preference order, not a filter on strength: a wolf that lists smell first


**L981**

> follows its nose past something it can see. An empty list notices everything and acts on none


**L982**

> of it.


**L990–997**

> Something made a sound; everything that can hear it takes note.
>
> Instant, unlike a scent: resolved where it happens rather than left lying about, so this emits
> the event and writes the alerts in one go.
>
> `loudness` scales the signal, which is how a stance makes the same footstep carry further.


**L1044–1047**

> What the party notices without being told — the "you hear something" line. Vaguer the fainter the
> signal: it reports that something is out there and roughly where, not a name and coordinates.


**L1057**

> Anything already in plain view is described by name elsewhere.


**L1065**

> What you can see is described elsewhere; this is for the rest.


**L1087**

> False for a trail or a trace rather than the thing itself.


**L1089**

> Minutes since it was noticed. Zero for something present now.


**L1093–1101**

> Everything one sense currently has to say, on demand.
>
> Different from {@link impressions}, which answers what is reaching the observer right now.
> Stopping to listen also reports what was already heard and not yet forgotten, so listening twice
> in a row is not silent.
>
> Strongest first, then by bearing, so the ordering is total.


**L1107**

> Keyed by bearing, because that is all the player is told: two creatures both to the north are


**L1108**

> one "something to the north".


**L1113**

> The clearer reading wins: present beats remembered, then stronger.


**L1140–1148**

> Which way something lies — the fuzzy reading.
>
> Coarser than the octant `bearing` in `grid/geometry.ts`: this one can say "nearby" and its
> diagonal band is wider.
>
> Returns a `systemText` key, not a word, so a `{direction}` placeholder reads in the module's own
> language.


### packages/engine/src/sim/spoils.ts


**L1–12**

> What the dead leave behind.
>
> Loot is rolled in the second transaction of a reduction rather than on the damage that killed the
> creature, for three reasons:
>
>   1. It sees the whole batch of events, so a creature killed by an ally's AI turn inside
>      `settle()` drops its loot too.
>   2. It has the corpse, so the map and the tile are read off it.
>   3. Rolling inside `adjustResource` would make the dice depend on where in an area-of-effect
>      each death happened, which is a reproducibility bug.


**L33–37**

> Extra draws earned by a scavenging skill: one for a success and two for a critical. The check is
> made here rather than inside `rollLoot`, which is shared verbatim with the editor's Balance
> preview and knows nothing about characters.


**L56–60**

> Drop what the newly dead were carrying, onto the ground where they fell. Deaths are handled in
> event order so the same batch always rolls the same dice, and each corpse gets its own sub-stream
> keyed by its id.


**L76**

> The creature's own table, then any table the finder additionally qualifies for.


**L77**

> `conditionalLoot` is drawn on top rather than instead.


**L95**

> A unique has now dropped; the flag removes it from every future draw, so it cannot be


**L96**

> found twice in one save.


### packages/engine/src/sim/temperament.ts


**L1–9**

> What a creature does when nothing is telling it what to do.
>
> Perception answers what it can tell is there; this answers what it does about it. A hound and a
> shopkeeper standing in the same doorway smell the same street, and only one goes to look.
>
> Every default here reproduces the engine's older behaviour: nothing wanders, nothing is leashed,
> and a fight ends the moment nobody can be perceived.


**L17**

> How one creature stands toward another. Exhaustive, and each exactly once.


**L20**

> How fast a creature moves, per reason it is moving.


**L28–32**

> A temperament with every default resolved and every distance in tiles. Built per statblock and
> cached, the way `SenseDef` is: resolving it touches three collections and it is read on every
> idle step of every creature.


**L34**

> Territory around the anchor, in tiles. Zero is a creature that stands still.


**L36**

> How far a lead may pull it from the anchor. `Infinity` when unbounded.


**L38**

> How far it chases before turning for home. `Infinity` when unbounded.


**L43**

> Senses it acts on, best first. Null means all of them, strongest first.


**L49**

> A temperament exactly as a module wrote it, before defaults are resolved.


**L64–68**

> How `observer` stands toward `other`. Built on {@link isHostileTo} rather than reading
> `disposition` directly, because disposition is held toward the party while this is a question
> about two particular creatures.


**L74–77**

> The temperament this creature runs on. Resolution mirrors `rangeOf`: the most specific statement
> wins, so a person's own entry beats the statblock they borrow to fight with.


**L110**

> An absent radius is no limit rather than a radius of zero: zero would pin a creature to the


**L111**

> tile it spawned on.


**L117**

> Resolved one name at a time, so a creature saying only how fast it wanders keeps the ruleset's


**L118**

> answer for the other three.


**L150–153**

> What two creatures sharing a temperament have in common. A person is keyed by who they are and a
> monster by what it is, so a room of twelve identical bog hounds resolves once.


**L160**

> Whether this creature registers that one at all.


### packages/engine/src/sim/terrain.ts


**L1–8**

> What the ground does to whoever is standing on it.
>
> `onEnter` and `onOccupy` are declared on every terrain in the schema and were
> read by nothing, so a lava tile was scenery. They live here rather than in
> the reducer because the end-of-turn hook is inside the combat turn, which the
> reducer imports — putting them together would be a cycle.


**L19–24**

> Run a terrain's own effects on whoever is standing on it.
>
> `onEnter` fires on the step; `onOccupy` at the end of a turn spent there.
> Both were declared on every terrain and read by nothing.


### packages/engine/src/sim/trade.ts


**L1–8**

> Buying and selling.
>
> Stock is derived, not stored. A shopkeeper's shelf is `rollLoot(seed, npc, day)`: a pure function
> of the run's seed, whose day it is, and what the party qualifies for. That means no new state, no
> migration, and a shop that restocks at dawn for free — the same derive-from-`state.minute` rule
> the scent trails follow. What has already been bought today is remembered in `flags`.


**L49**

> Which in-world day it is, which is how often a shop turns over.

**L212**

### packages/engine/src/sim/traps.ts


**L1–12**

> Traps: finding them, springing them, and taking them apart.
>
> A trap lives on the map beside the gates, keyed by the same packed tile integer, and moves
> through four states: `hidden` until somebody searches, `found` once they have, then `disarmed` or
> `sprung`.
>
> There is deliberately no passive detection roll on movement. A roll re-made on every step finds
> everything eventually and teaches the player nothing; making `search` the only way to find a trap
> is deterministic, gives `search` a job inside a dungeon, and leaves walking into one a real
> consequence of not looking.


**L43**

> Write one trap's state back, leaving the rest of the map alone.


**L58–61**

> Step onto a tile that may be trapped. A trap that has been found is still a trap, which is why
> disarming exists. Monsters spring traps too — the trap does not know who is standing on it.


**L75**

> `target` is the one who stepped on it, which is what an authored trap means by `{ "ref":


**L76**

> "target.id" }`.


**L81**

> A reusable trap re-arms, but it is no longer a secret once it has gone off.


**L85–88**

> Look for what is hidden underfoot. Returns whether anything was in range at all, so the caller
> can tell "you find nothing here" from "there was nothing to find".


**L98**

> Sorted, so two searches of the same ground roll in the same order.


**L120**

> A trap this character has found and can reach, nearest first.


**L136–139**

> Take a found trap apart. Failing costs time; only a fumble sets it off, since a trap that
> punished every failed attempt would make disarming strictly worse than walking around.


**L170**

> Fumbling sets it off under the hands of whoever was working on it.


### packages/engine/src/sim/triggers.ts


**L1–10**

> Triggers: things that happen because you went somewhere.
>
> The interesting part is repetition. A set piece fires once; an ambush can fire every visit; a
> ritual repeats until it is completed; ambience loops on a cooldown; a shifting barrow resets
> itself. All five are the same declaration with a different `mode`.
>
> `remember` decides whether the world keeps a record, which is what makes a ransacked shrine stay
> ransacked across visits.


**L34**

> Where a trigger lives, so `restart` knows what to reset.


**L36**

> Content id of the place or thing the triggers hang off.


**L41–44**

> Whether a trigger should fire now. Separated from firing so the decision can be tested on its own
> — the modes are where the bugs live.


**L56**

> Only ever once for this save, which is what `remember` records.


**L65**

> Repeats until its completion condition holds, then stops for good.


**L84**

> Chance is rolled last, so a trigger that fails its gate does not consume randomness and shift


**L85**

> everything downstream.


**L91–94**

> Fire the triggers on a place for one occasion. `on` names the occasion — entering, resting,
> finishing a fight — and only triggers declaring that occasion are considered.


**L111**

> A mod gets the last word on whether a module trigger fires. `replace` decides it outright;


**L112**

> anything else just observes.


**L125**

> A restart wipes what the world remembered about this place, so returning to an unfinished


**L126**

> barrow finds it reordered rather than half-cleared.


**L152–156**

> Forget that a place's triggers ever fired. Trigger ids are namespaced by their source in
> practice, but a module may reuse one, so the restarting trigger itself is kept — otherwise a
> `restart` would immediately be eligible to fire again.


**L167**

> Collect the triggers that apply to a place, biome first so ambience is shared.

### packages/engine/src/state.ts


**L1–10**

> Game state.
>
> Everything here is plain, serializable data — no class instances, no functions, no `Date`. A save
> file is this object, RNG state included, so seed plus action log reproduces a run exactly.
>
> Store base facts, compute the rest. Attributes and current resource values are stored because
> nothing else determines them. Maxima, Guard and initiative are computed on demand, because they
> depend on equipment and active conditions.

> drift.

> serialization.

> misspelling is catchable.
>

> every level, so two runs that learn the same things in a different order compare equal.

>

> state for a mod that is not installed.
>

> `canonical()`, which sorts keys at every level, so write order does not matter. Mods only ever
> write under their own id, enforced host-side.

### packages/engine/src/stats.ts


**L1–16**

> Deriving a character's numbers from the module's formulas.
>
> Nothing here knows what an attribute means. It reads `rules.attributes`, evaluates each one's
> `modifier` expression, then feeds the results to the resource and derived-stat formulas.
>
> Evaluation order is a contract, because the formulas reference each other:
>
>   1. `attributes` — stored on the entity, depends on nothing
>   2. `mod`        — from each attribute's `modifier`, sees only `value`
>   3. `max`        — resource maxima, may see `attr`, `mod`, `level`
>   4. `derived`    — may additionally see `res` and `max`
>
> A resource maximum therefore cannot depend on a derived stat, so a module cannot express a cycle
> that could only be caught at runtime.


**L28**

> Formulas must not consume randomness; a stat that changes when re-read is a bug.


**L47–54**

> The thresholds a requirement compares against: mastery tiers and faction ranks.
>
> `compileRequirement` lowers "requires journeyman smithing" to a comparison against
> `tiers.journeyman`, and "requires the Wardens to trust you" to one against
> `ranks.wardens.trusted`. Both are pure functions of the module, so they are built once and cached
> on the module object.


**L66**

> Scoped per faction, so two factions may each name a rank `trusted`.


**L122**

> Step 2: attribute modifiers. Each `modifier` sees only `{ value }`.


**L135**

> The scope a formula sees before maxima are known.


**L150**

> Step 3: resource maxima.


**L164–167**

> Step 3, the other end: resource minima. Sees exactly what `max` sees, for the same reason — a
> minimum that depended on a derived stat could express a cycle.


**L181**

> What a resource starts at. Defaults to full, which is what `initial` being optional means.


**L201–204**

> Additive modifiers contributed by equipped items and active conditions. Computed each time rather
> than stored, because equipping a shield or catching fire changes them.


**L223**

> And whatever its declared `properties` are worth.


**L242**

> What those conditions imply counts too, at magnitude 1: an implied condition was never applied,


**L243**

> so there is no magnitude to inherit.


**L255**

> Step 4: derived stats, including gear and condition modifiers.


**L278**

> Every computed number for an entity, in dependency order.


**L292–305**

> Namespaces where a missing key means "not yet" rather than "typo".
>
> Flags, quests and memory are written during play and are legitimately empty at the start.
> Anything structural (`actor.attr`, `actor.derived`) stays strict, so a misspelling there is an
> error rather than a silent zero.
>
> `tiers` and `ranks` are not open: they are derived from the module rather than written during
> play, so a missing key is a misspelled tier.
>
> `lore` is open and `threads` is not: a lore id is absent from state until the party learns it,
> exactly as a flag is, while a thread is declared by the module and `threadScope` populates every
> one.


**L308–311**

> The module's proficiency-style bonus for a character, if it declares one: a single formula over
> `actor.level`.


**L320–328**

> A character's effective rank in a skill: what they trained, plus what they wear.
>
> `entity.skills` is written at character creation and at monster spawn and never again, so
> equipment is the only way a rank moves during play. Summed here rather than at the two call
> sites, so a check and a `minRank` gate cannot disagree.
>
> Only equipped items count.


**L340**

> Every skill the module declares, at the rank this entity brings to it.


**L349**

> Everything an entity is carrying, by declared weight.


**L359–363**

> The world half of the scope: the clock, and what the module makes of it. Phase, month and year
> are derived on every read rather than stored, so content can gate on `world.phase` without
> anything having to tick.


**L376**

> Null underground, where there is no daylight to have a phase of.


**L382**

> Whether a character's class is trained in a saving throw.


**L389**

> The evaluation context the engine uses everywhere.


**L394–398**

> The scope handed to the DSL during play. Every readable value content can reference lives here,
> which is why the DSL needs no game-specific primitives: `flags.met_vess` and `actor.res.vitality`
> are ordinary paths.


**L399–403**

> The other side of a roll, as a predicate can read it. Narrower than `buildScope`: what an
> attacker or a reacting creature may know about the one in front of them, and nothing about the

### packages/engine/src/world/dungeon.ts


**L1–31**

> Dungeon generation.
>
> Rooms are placed, connected into a tree, carved into tiles, and then locked. Two properties hold
> for every seed, by construction:
>
>   Everything is reachable. Rooms are connected with a spanning tree before any extra loops are
> added.
>
>   Every key lies before its lock. A lock is only placed on a tree edge, which splits the dungeon
> in two, and the key goes in the part containing the entrance. Because the edge is a bridge, there
> is no other route to the far side.
>
> Every extra edge — branchiness loops and the `minExits`-raising connections — is added only after
> locks are chosen, and refused when it would join a lock's two sides.
>
> Rng streams. Each subsystem derives its own, so a change in one cannot shift another; template
> `requires` evaluation is isolated per template (`qualify:<id>`) so adding a gate to one room
> cannot reshuffle the map:
>
>   `count`          roomCount roll
>   `size`           authored width/height rolls
>   `qualify:<id>`   template requirement evaluation
>   `rooms`          placement sampling
>   `corridors`      elbow orientation, winding walks, per-edge length rolls
>   `locks`          lock selection and key hosts
>   `loops`          branchiness extras
>   `scatter`        palette scatter over the carved floor
>
> `depth` and `populate` are derived by the caller.

### packages/engine/src/world/dungeon/bsp.ts


**L1–13**

> BSP interiors: the made place.
>
> Recursive splits carve the bounds into leaves that tile it completely, every room sharing its
> walls with its neighbours — the floor plan of a building, where rooms-and-corridors reads as a
> warren.
>
> The recursion's structure is the spanning tree: each split connects its two halves through one
> door punched in the shared wall, so connectivity and the key-before-lock argument hold for the
> same reason as in the rooms algorithm.

> `corridorLength` is naturally inert here, since doors join rooms directly.

> Corridor carving, in three styles.

> because a crossing is where a door belongs. Width carves a broader brush along the run, but a

### packages/engine/src/world/dungeon/degree.ts


**L1–18**

> Room degree: how many ways in and out of a room there are.
>
> `roomTemplates[].minExits`/`maxExits` are applied here:
>
>   - the spanning tree refuses to attach new rooms through a room already at its `maxExits`, so a
>     `maxExits: 1` boss cell ends up a leaf;
>   - after locking, rooms under their `minExits` get extra nearest-neighbour connections, without
>     ever bypassing a lock;
>   - branchiness loops respect `maxExits` too.
>
> Connectivity always wins. When every connected room is saturated the cap is relaxed on the
> lowest-degree one, because a dungeon you cannot cross is worse than a room with one door too
> many.
>
> Everything here is deterministic — nearest-neighbour with index tie-breaks — so it consumes no
> rng.


**L33–36**

> Prim-style spanning tree over room centres, honouring `maxExits`. Identical to a plain nearest-
> neighbour tree when no template declares a cap, since every room defaults to Infinity.


**L56**

> The relaxed candidate ignores caps entirely, preferring the lowest-degree attachment


**L57**

> point so a forced violation lands where it does least harm. Ties break on distance then


**L58**

> index.


**L83**

> Degree of every node under a set of edges.


**L93–97**

> Extra edges that raise every under-`minExits` room toward its floor. Runs after locking, so a
> candidate that would join the two sides of a locked door is rejected the same way branchiness
> loops are. An unsatisfiable minimum is left short.


**L103**

> Whether the algorithm can physically connect this pair (BSP: adjacency).


**L114**

> Nearest room with headroom that is not already a neighbour and does not bridge a lock.


**L115**

> Deterministic: distance, then index.


### packages/engine/src/world/dungeon/rooms.ts


**L1–9**

> Room placement for the rooms-and-corridors algorithm.
>
> Rejection sampling, as ever — simple, predictable, and it reads naturally
> when rooms vary a lot in size. What changed: the gap between rooms is no
> longer a hardcoded single tile. `dungeons[].corridorLength` now sets the
> spacing rooms are sampled at (its mean, statically — no rng is consumed
> computing it), so "long corridors" finally means long corridors.


**L41**

> Fixed dimensions, for templates whose map is a static `world.maps` entry.


**L53–61**

> The mean of a dice expression, statically.
>
> Used for room spacing so that map geometry does not consume the rng — a
> corridorLength of `2d3` spaces rooms four tiles apart on every seed, and
> only the winding walk (which has its own stream) varies. Keep clauses are
> approximated as keeping plain dice; nobody writes `4d6kh3` corridors, and if
> they do, "roughly the mean" is still the honest reading.


**L80**

> Rectangles do not overlap, and keep `spacing` tiles between their walls.


**L90**

> Place non-overlapping rooms by rejection sampling.


**L98**

> Size for a template that declares no map spec, from the dungeon.


**L104–110**

> Build the draw order up front.
>
> A guaranteed role means *exactly one* — one entrance, one boss. Drawing
> purely by weight would give a dungeon three entrances and two boss rooms,
> which is what happens without this.


**L119**

> Everything else is drawn from templates that do not claim a unique role.


**L133**

> A hand-authored room uses its own dimensions, glyph layout or static map.


**L146**

> A filler room drawn from a role-bearing template loses that role, so


**L147**

> the guarantee holds even when a module has only one template.

### packages/engine/src/world/staticmap.ts


**L1–17**

> Building a hand-authored map, exactly as written.
>
> A static map is the author's promise that a place is *this*, not "something
> like this": the same castle on every seed, in every run. Accordingly this
> file consumes **no rng anywhere** — everything it produces is a pure
> function of the module — and it never "fixes" the map. Where `buildMap`
> guarantees a connected floor by digging, a disconnected static map is the
> author's decision (or the linter's warning), never silently re-carved.
>
> The layer kinds compose into the existing `MapInstance` shape: terrain
> layers composite into the tile array (last non-empty cell wins, so a base
> layer carries the ground and later layers lay roads over it), object layers
> become the packed-key records the instance already has, and marker layers
> become named positions — `entry` for arrival, `door` for where corridors may
> attach when the map is stamped into a dungeon.


**L32**

> Where the party arrives: the entry marker, or the first passable tile.


**L34**

### packages/library/src/claim.ts


**L1–14**

> One tab at a time, per world.
>
> The studio holds a world assembled in memory and writes back the files that changed, working out
> which by diffing against a snapshot of what it last wrote. That snapshot lives in the tab.
>
> With two tabs the failure is not a lost edit but two tabs each writing a different subset of the
> same world's files, producing a tree that is half of one document and half of another — and every
> individual file is valid, so no diagnostic describes it.
>
> So the second tab does not save. Web Locks are held for as long as the callback's promise is
> pending, which is the rest of the page's life, and the browser releases them when the tab goes —
> including on a crash, which is why this is a lock and not a flag in the store.

### packages/library/src/gzip.ts


**L1–13**

> Compression, at the two edges that need it.
>
> Worlds are stored compressed and shipped compressed. Aurendel is 2.9 MB of
> pretty JSON, 1.5 MB minified and 296 KB gzipped — a tenth of the storage
> quota and a tenth of the transfer, for a few milliseconds of CPU.
>
> Everything goes through here rather than reaching for `CompressionStream` at
> a call site, so that moving this work to a Worker later is a change to one
> file. It also keeps the codec a recorded fact: a record says how it was
> compressed, so a browser without `CompressionStream` can store plain bytes
> and still be read by one that has it.


**L15**

> The two bytes every gzip stream starts with.


**L20**

> Is compression available here? Everything current has it; Node 18+ too.


**L25**

> Does this look like a gzip stream?


**L30–37**

> Push bytes through a compression stream and collect the result.
>
> Typed as `GenericTransformStream` because that is what the DOM declares
> `CompressionStream` and `DecompressionStream` to be — their `writable` takes
> a `BufferSource`, not a `Uint8Array`, so the narrower `TransformStream` does
> not describe them.


**L57**

> A value, minified and compressed. Returns the codec actually used.


**L64–71**

> The inverse, tolerant of either codec.
>
> Tolerant on purpose: a static host that infers `Content-Encoding: gzip` from
> a `.gz` extension will have inflated the body already, and a decompressor
> handed plain JSON throws. Sniffing the magic bytes costs two comparisons and
> removes a failure that only ever reproduces on someone else's deployment.

## `packages/mods/src`

_95 comments_


### packages/mods/src/directives.ts


**L1–15**

> What a mod hands back.
>
> Mods never mutate state directly. They return requests, which the host feeds through the engine's
> own doors — `applyOps` for effect ops, the transaction for patches and events. That is not a
> restriction on what a mod may change; `patch` can write anywhere in `GameState`. It is about
> there being one place where mod output enters the engine.
>
> Two rules are enforced on the way through, and neither is a game rule:
>
>   - JSON-safety and determinism. `NaN` and `±Infinity` are rejected, because `JSON.stringify`
>     turns them into `null` — a state carrying `NaN` would compare equal under `statesEqual` while
>     behaving differently.
>   - Size. A mod that stuffs a map into `modState` makes every save unusable.


**L19**

> A path into `GameState`, e.g. `['entities', 'e:1', 'hp']`.


**L30**

> Effect ops, run through `applyOps` exactly as the module DSL's are.


**L32**

> Direct state writes. The unrestricted path.


**L34**

> A `custom` game event, visible to triggers, quests, and other mods.


**L42**

> Refuse the action being hooked, with a keyed reason.


**L51–54**

> Put a line in the transcript. Keyed rather than literal: the text comes from the mod's own
> `systemText`, so a mod's prose is data the same way a module's is.


**L60**

> How it reads: ordinary prose, a system note, or a refusal-coloured line.


**L64**

> Write into this mod's own namespaced bag on `GameState`.


**L72**

> Only meaningful from a `replace` handler: run the core implementation after all.


**L78**

> A handler returns any number of directives, or nothing at all.


**L81–85**

> Reject values that survive `JSON.stringify` in a shape that lies. `NaN` and `Infinity` serialize
> to `null`, so a state holding one compares equal to a state holding the other under `statesEqual`
> while behaving differently on reload. Functions and `undefined` vanish entirely.


**L115**

> Bytes a single mod may hold in `state.modState`.

### packages/mods/src/editor.ts


**L1–20**

> The editor mod contract.
>
> An engine mod changes how a game plays; an editor mod changes how a game is authored. They are
> separate targets because they run against different hosts, and a feature normally ships as one of
> each — an editor mod adds a field, and its paired engine mod reads it.
>
> Why widgets are described rather than rendered
>
> Mod code runs inside QuickJS, which has no DOM and no React, so it returns a description of a
> component and the host renders it with the editor's own components. That covers extra fields,
> tables, buttons and text. It cannot express a canvas, a map overlay, a graph, drag-and-drop, or
> per-keystroke feedback; an editor mod that needs those needs a core editor change.
>
> Why fields write into `extra`
>
> `schema/common.ts` documents `extra` as the supported way to exceed what the format ships with. A
> mod adding `morale` to a monster writes into that bag, so the module still validates and hashes
> stably against a stock engine.


**L24**

> Editor hooks. Smaller than the engine's, and for a different job.


**L26**

> Extra validation over the whole document, surfaced in the problems console.


**L28**

> Extra fields on whatever the author has selected.


**L30**

> Named actions offered in the Mods dock.


**L32**

> A panel the mod draws, refreshed on every interaction.


**L46–49**

> A field description. A subset of the editor's own `FieldSpec`, which is already plain data, so
> the existing `Field` component renders a mod's field with no new rendering code.

### packages/mods/src/hash.ts


**L1–12**

> Mod content hashing.
>
> The primitive takes a **file map**, not a directory, and that is the whole
> trick: the on-disk folder and a packed single-file bundle produce the same
> tag, so a mod can be shared either way and still satisfy the hash a game
> pinned. `load.ts` only builds the map; it never hashes a directory.
>
> Ordering is fixed here rather than inherited from the filesystem, mirroring
> the sorted discovery in `@dm/module/load` — a hash that depended on
> `readdir` order would differ between machines for identical content.


**L16**

> posix-relative path within the mod directory to file contents.


**L19**

> The manifest file name, excluded from the file list because it is hashed separately.


**L22–26**

> Field separator. A NUL rather than a space, because a space can appear in a
> path and the point of a separator is that it cannot. Built with
> `fromCharCode` so this source stays plain ASCII.


**L29–36**

> Content tag over a mod.
>
> The manifest's own `hash` field is removed before hashing, because it cannot
> describe a text it is part of. Each file then contributes its path, its
> length, and its contents: the length is what stops contents that happen to
> look like a path boundary from forging a different layout with the same tag.


### packages/mods/src/index.ts


**L1–7**

> The mod format and its sandbox.
>
> Browser-safe: nothing here touches the filesystem. Reading a mod off disk
> lives in `@dm/mods/load`, which is deliberately not re-exported so a client
> import of it is a build error.


**L48**

> The editor target. Separate from the engine contract because the two run


**L49**

> against different hosts and share nothing but the manifest.


### packages/mods/src/load.ts


**L1–19**

> Reading mods off disk.
>
> Node-only, and published as `@dm/mods/load` rather than from the index, so a client import of
> this file is a build error — the same arrangement `@dm/module/load` uses.
>
> Layout, mirroring how modules are directories:
>
>     mods/engine/<id>-<hash>/mod.json
>     mods/engine/<id>-<hash>/main.js
>     mods/engine/<id>-<hash>/lib/anything.js
>     mods/editor/<id>-<hash>/...
>
> The folder name is the mod's address and `mod.json` has to agree with it, checked the way
> `readAssembledModule` checks a map folder's id.
>
> Every directory walk is sorted, so the file map — and therefore the hash — never depends on
> filesystem enumeration order.


**L32**

> Relative to the mod directory, e.g. `mod.json`.


**L48**

> Recomputed from the files. Never the manifest's own claim.


**L54–57**

> A mod that could not be read at all. Kept separate from `LoadedModFromDisk` rather than served as
> one with a null manifest: a mod whose manifest failed to parse has no id, no entry and no hooks.


**L67**

> Files a mod directory never contributes.


**L70**

> Extensions worth reading. Anything else is not something the host can use.


**L87**

> List mod folder names under one target, sorted.


**L96**

> Read one mod directory. Never throws: problems come back as issues.


**L153**

> The folder name is the address. A mismatch means tooling and the manifest disagree about which


**L154**

> mod this is.


**L173**

> Drift warns rather than blocks. The hash sits in a file the author can edit, so refusing to


**L174**

> load only teaches people to edit the hash.


**L189**

> Directories that hold something mod-shaped but could not be read.


**L193–197**

> Read every mod under a root, both targets, in sorted order. Unreadable mods are reported rather

> installed.

### packages/mods/src/registry.test.ts


**L1–7**

> Resolution and ordering.
>
> Load order is behaviour, so "the same everywhere" is a correctness property
> rather than a nicety — two players running the same mods must get the same
> game.


**L114**

> A fixed rotation per iteration: deterministic, and enough to prove the


**L115**

> result does not depend on input order.


### packages/mods/src/registry.ts


**L1–11**

> Resolution and ordering.
>
> Two jobs, both of which have to be identical in the editor and the play app:
>
>   1. Decide which declared mods will run, and say why any of them will not.
>   2. Put them in an order that is the same on every machine.
>
> Ordering is the subtle half: load order changes behaviour, so it must not depend on `readdir`, on
> insertion, or on a `Map`'s iteration.


**L16**

> One entry from the game document's `mods` section.


**L39**

> False only when a required mod is missing, or two required mods collide.


**L41**

> In final order: declaration order, then `loadAfter`.


**L50–59**

> Resolve a game's declared mods against what is installed.
>
> Two policies:
>
>   - A missing required mod blocks play. The game says it needs it, and pretending otherwise
>     produces a broken session that looks like an engine bug.
>   - A hash mismatch warns and loads anyway. The hash lives in a JSON file the player can edit, so
>     a hard block teaches people to edit the hash rather than fix the mismatch.


**L97**

> Prefer the exact pinned build; several versions of one mod can sit side by side because the


**L98**

> folder name carries the hash.


**L115**

> Required mods are forced on: the toggle is not consulted.


**L158–166**

> Deterministic order.
>
> The base sequence is the order the game document declares: stable across machines, part of the
> module hash, and something an author can fix by reordering. Ties break on the mod id, bytewise.
>
> `loadAfter` is then applied as a stable topological sort seeded by that sequence, so a constraint
> reorders only what it has to.


**L197**

> Kahn, always taking the earliest still-available id in the base sequence, which is what makes


**L198**

> the result stable rather than merely valid.


**L220**

> Fall back to the base order so callers still get something to show; `ok` is already false.


**L227–235**

> Two mods replacing the same hook.
>
> Highest priority wins, then the earlier of the deterministic order. The loser is reported rather
> than silently skipped.
>
> The exception is two required mods claiming the same replace: the game insists on both, and
> silently picking one is worse than saying so.


**L287**

> `<id>-<hash>` for every active mod, sorted — what a save records.
