### packages/module/src/schema/module.ts


**L1–7**

> The module document: an entire game as one JSON file.
>
> This is the unit that gets authored, validated, shared and played. A save records the module id,
> version and content hash it was created against, so loading a save into a module that has since
> changed fails loudly.


**L17**

> The module format's own version, bumped when the schema breaks compatibility.


**L26**

> Free-form, for the editor's gallery.


**L31**

> How a character is built at the start of a game.


**L34**

> Points to spend across attributes.


**L36**

> Cost of raising an attribute to each value; index is the target score.


**L40**

> Restrict the choices offered; empty means everything is allowed.


**L56**

> How many characters the player controls.


**L59**

> Where play begins: a region for story mode, or a dungeon to dive straight in.


**L63**

> Text-grammar pool for the opening scene.


**L65**

> How the rest of the party keeps up with whoever is leading.


**L68**

> Beyond this many tiles, a follower hurries.


**L70**

> Steps a hurrying follower takes in one go.


**L75**

> Flags set on a new game.


**L77**

> Ends the game when it becomes true.


**L80–88**

> What finishing an `isEnding` arc does. `end` stops the run; `continue` records the win,
> narrates it, and leaves the world live so a module can put content on the far side of its own
> ending.
>
> `.optional()`, and it must stay that way: `compileModule` hashes `parsed.data`, so a
> `.default('end')` would insert the key into every document zod has ever parsed, change every
> module's content hash, and make `load()` refuse every existing save.


**L93–100**

> A mod this game expects, pinned to the exact build it was authored against.
>
> `id` and `hash` are separate fields rather than one `"thorns-3f2a…"` string because
> `mergeModules` merges by `id`: a combined string would make a base pinning one version and a
> patch pinning another look like two different mods, and both would survive the merge. Split,
> `$delete` also works — a pack drops an inherited mod with `{ "id": "thorns", "$delete": true }`.


**L104**

> The build this game was authored against. Drift warns; it does not block.


**L107**

> Forced on, and play is blocked when it is missing.


**L109**

> Shown when the mod is missing or drifted — the author's own words.


**L118**

> Format version, so a future reader knows how to migrate this document.


**L122**

> Engine range this module expects, e.g. `^1.0.0`.


**L124–127**

> Base module to layer on, as `id@version`. The named module is loaded first and this document
> is merged over it, so a pack can ship twelve monsters instead of forking an entire game.


**L133–143**

> Mods this game expects.
>
> `.optional()`, and it must stay that way: `compileModule` hashes `parsed.data`, so a
> `.default([])` would insert `mods: []` into every document zod has ever parsed and change
> every module's hash, and `load()` refuses a save whose recorded hash no longer matches.
> `compile.test.ts` pins the shipped modules' hashes to catch that.
>
> The section does participate in the hash once an author adds it, so changing a required mod
> pin surfaces as drift on an old save rather than passing silently.


**L154**

> A module as authored, before compilation resolves and indexes it.


**L160**

> Every addressable collection, as `section.collection` paths.


**L209**

> `narrative.memory` is deliberately absent: it is a settings block edited as one section rather


**L210**

> than a collection of independently addressable entries, and its `rules` list is nested inside


**L211**

> it.


### packages/module/src/schema/narrative.ts


**L1–6**

> Narrative: the text grammar, dialogue, and quests. With no LLM at runtime, prose comes from
> weighted template pools keyed on world state. `textGrammar` is that vocabulary; the narrator
> expands it with a scene-seeded RNG, so a room reads the same on re-entry but differs between
> playthroughs.


**L15–18**

> One way of saying something. Variants may carry conditions, so a room reads differently once it
> has been looted or its occupants are dead.


**L21**

> Supports `{placeholder}` interpolation resolved against the scene scope.


**L24**

> Say it differently once they know you, or fear you.


**L31–34**

> A named pool of phrasings. The compiler warns when a pool is small enough that players will
> notice the repetition.


**L43–47**

> One thing the player can say. Options are gated by the same requirement vocabulary as everything
> else, so a line can appear only for a certain class, only while carrying proof, or only if this
> NPC has not heard what you did.


**L54**

> Shown greyed out with this reason instead of hidden.


**L57**

> A persuasion or intimidation attempt: the NPC resists.


**L61–64**

> The number to beat, as a formula. Standing belongs here rather than in `requires`:
> someone you have wronged should be harder to talk round, not impossible to talk to.


**L66**

> The NPC's own roll opposes it, rather than a fixed number.


**L74**

> Selectable once, ever.


**L76**

> Omit to end the conversation.


**L84**

> What the NPC says; picked from a pool so repeat visits vary.


**L86**

> Runs when the node is first reached.


**L88**

> Reaching this node is itself remembered, so later talk can refer back.


**L90**

> Skip to another node when the party already qualifies.


**L110**

> A `goto` into nowhere would strand the player mid-conversation.


**L122**

> Every branch an option can take must land somewhere real, including the success and


**L123**

> failure outcomes of a persuasion check.


**L141**

> One step of a quest. Objectives observe the event stream.


**L146**

> Common shapes, so the usual objective needs no hand-written predicate.


**L148**

> Target of the objective, interpreted per `kind`.


**L151–154**

> For `custom`, the completion condition, and required. For the other kinds, an extra gate the
> matching event must also satisfy — not a second way to finish.


**L156**

> Additional gate before the objective becomes active.


**L158**

> Hidden objectives do not appear in the journal until complete.


**L171**

> Becomes available when this holds.


**L173**

> Starts automatically rather than needing to be accepted.


**L176**

> Gate on development, items, factions, memory, or other quests.


**L178–181**

> Stages run in order; each holds its own objectives. A single-stage quest is just a list of
> objectives.


**L192**

> Prose shown while this stage is the active one.


**L199**

> Objectives must be completed in order.


**L204**

> Fails the quest when this becomes true, e.g. the giver dies.


**L220**

> Quests unlocked on completion, forming a chain.


**L222**

> What the world remembers about this, and who tells whom.


**L224**

> Can be taken again once finished.


**L226**

> Fails automatically this many in-world days after starting.


**L231**

> A quest with neither stages nor objectives can never be completed.


**L248–257**

> One thing the party can come to know.
>
> A quest records what you were told to do; lore records what you found out. Content teaches an
> entry with the `learnLore` effect from anywhere: a line of dialogue, a trigger on arriving
> somewhere, an object picked up off a corpse.
>
> Learning is permanent and unordered, so an entry says only what it is. Where it sits in a story
> is the thread's business.


**L261**

> The clue itself, in the words the player reads it in.


**L263**

> Longer context, when a single line is not enough.


**L266–269**

> Vary the wording instead of fixing it, for entries a player may meet more than one way. Takes
> precedence over `name` when the journal has a scene to expand it against.


**L271**

> Where it came from — "Netmender Ossa, Sarnport", "cut into the lintel".


**L276–279**

> A heading several lore entries hang under, so a partial answer reads as one. The same shape as
> {@link arcSchema}. Declaring the whole set is what lets the journal say three of five.


**L285**

> In reading order, not discovery order.


**L290**

> A story arc: a gated sequence of quests.


**L297**

> Reaching this ends the game.


**L302–305**

> How a deed is weighted when it reaches a faction's ears. The social sim reads these rather than
> hardcoding what counts as an outrage.


**L311**

> Standing change for the faction that cares, before witness decay.


**L313**

> Which faction is affected; omit to derive it from the victim.


**L315**

> How memorable it is, as a multiplier on decay.


**L317**

> How much the story mutates as it spreads, 0..1.


**L324–327**

> How many nodes a dialogue may auto-advance through before it stops. A loop guard, and a cap
> on how long a scene can run without the player saying anything.


**L330–333**

> What the engine itself says: refusals, combat lines, the words a narrated sentence is
> assembled from. The engine holds no prose of its own.


**L338–345**

> What the party can find out, and the headings it hangs under.
>
> `.optional()` rather than `.default([])`, unlike every collection above: `compileModule`
> hashes the document after zod applies defaults, and `load` refuses a save whose recorded
> module hash has moved, so a new defaulted top-level field would break every save ever
> written. `collectionAt` and `CompiledModule.all` both read a missing collection as empty.


**L349–352**

> Memory, gossip, forgetting and learning. Defaults give a world that runs itself; `mode:
> "manual"` hands control back to the content.


### packages/module/src/schema/requirement.test.ts


**L7**

> Parse through the schema so defaults are applied, as a real module would.


**L14**

> A party member and world state to evaluate gates against.


**L28**

> Supplied by the engine's `buildScope`: the rank a tier starts at, and each


**L29**

> faction's own ladder. Both are scoped exactly as the engine scopes them.


**L81**

> An untrained skill must read as rank 0, not as an error.


**L86**

> lockpicking is rank 3, and `adept` starts at rank 3.


**L89**

> lore is rank 1 — trained, but not yet adept.


**L95**

> A tier compiled against an unpopulated namespace resolved to null and


**L96**

> then threw inside the comparison, from call sites that do not catch —


**L97**

> so a gate, an ability, a quest or a dialogue option carrying a `minTier`


**L98**

> took the whole reduction down.


**L138**

> An unknown faction reads as neutral rather than failing.


**L143**

> Standing with the wardens is 20: past `friend` (10), short of `trusted` (25).


**L149**

> Both ladders name a `trusted` rank at different standings; asking about


**L150**

> the wardens must not be answered by the thieves' threshold.


**L176**

> The deed was on day 4 and it is now day 9.


**L182**

> "Requirements (lack of, or completed)" — absence is as common as presence.


**L206**

> The door opened by the key *or* by picking the lock.


### packages/module/src/schema/requirement.ts


**L1–14**

> Requirements — the one gating vocabulary.
>
> Loot limited by level and mastery, doors that need a key or a spell, dialogue that only appears
> once a faction trusts you, quests that unlock when another finishes, creatures that react to what
> they remember: all the same question — does this actor, right now, meet these conditions?
>
> Defined once and reused everywhere, which gives three things:
>
>   - The editor renders one gate UI in every context.
>   - `compileRequirement` lowers it to a DSL {@link Predicate}, so gates need no special
>     evaluator.
>   - Every clause supports "lack of" as well as "has".


**L21**

> Quest states a requirement can test.


**L27**

> Minimum rank. Ranks are the module's mastery scale.


**L29**

> Named tier from `rules.masteryTiers`, if the module uses them.


**L38**

> Consume the item when the gate opens, as a key is spent.


**L40**

> Must be worn or wielded, not merely carried.


**L49**

> Require a specific objective rather than the whole quest.


**L59**

> Named rank from the faction's own ladder.


**L64–67**

> A memory condition. Memory is per-NPC and imperfect, so a requirement can ask whether this NPC
> knows about a deed rather than whether it happened.


**L71**

> Whose memory to inspect; defaults to the NPC in the conversation.


**L73**

> False tests that they have not heard about it.


**L75**

> Only count deeds within this many in-world days.


**L83**

> Omit to test merely that the flag is set.


**L88–92**

> Something the party has found out. Distinct from a flag because a lore id is declared: the
> journal can list what is still missing, and the linter can prove the clue is teachable. `known:
> false` gates a rumour on not having heard it already.


**L108**

> The clauses shared by a requirement and by each of its alternatives.


**L115**

> Character development: which classes and ancestries qualify.


**L122–124**

> What the actor is — "this only affects undead", "this line only appears if you speak Dwarvish".


**L129**

> Coin in the party's purse — what makes a toll gate a toll.


**L137–142**

> `.optional()` where its neighbours default to `[]`: a requirement is nested in hundreds of
> places, so defaulting this would write `lore: []` into every gate in every module and move
> every module hash, which `load` reads as a different module. The `clause()` helper below reads
> undefined as empty.


**L145**

> Conditions that must NOT hold — "only before you have met her".


**L154**

> `.optional()` for the hash reason given on the positive clause above.


**L160**

> Escape hatch for anything the structured clauses cannot express.


**L164**

> One alternative branch. Flat, so the type and the JSON Schema stay finite.


**L167–173**

> A gate. Every clause is optional; those present must all hold, and `anyOf` offers alternatives.
>
> Alternatives are one level deep rather than fully recursive: arbitrary nesting would make the
> type circular and the generated form unbounded, and `custom` covers the rare gate that needs
> more.


**L184**

> Alternatives are absent on a branch, so treat that uniformly.


**L189–192**

> Read a clause list defensively. Zod fills these with `[]`, but the editor, the linter and the
> preview simulations work on raw authored JSON where an unused clause is absent.


**L197**

> The `without` block, likewise, may be missing entirely.


**L219**

> True when nothing is gated, so callers can skip evaluation entirely.


**L253–257**

> Lower a requirement to a DSL predicate. Structured gates are what authors edit; predicates are
> what the engine runs, so gates need no separate evaluator. The scope paths below are the contract
> the engine must supply.


**L266**

> Class and ancestry lists are alternatives: any of them qualifies.


**L288**

> A tier is a named rung on the rank ladder, per `masteryTier.atRank`, so the comparison is the


**L289**

> skill's rank against the rank the tier starts at. `tiers` comes from the engine's scope.


**L303**

> Every named tongue must be spoken, not merely one of them.


**L324**

> Ranks are thresholds on the faction's own standing, scoped per faction so two factions may


**L325**

> each declare a rank called `trusted`.


**L337**

> `memory.<who>.<deedKind>` is how the engine exposes what is remembered.


**L347**

> `lore.<id>` is the minute it was learned, so `exists` is the whole test — and it must be


**L348**

> `exists` rather than `test`, since a clue learned in the first minute stores a 0.


**L354**

> Negations.


### packages/module/src/schema/rules.ts


**L1–7**

> The ruleset, defined entirely in data.
>
> The engine does not know what Might is, that hit points exist, that armour class is `10 +
> agility`, or that checks roll a d20. All of it is declared here and evaluated through the DSL, so
> a module can ship a dice-pool system or six alien attributes without engine changes.


**L28–31**

> A character attribute. `modifier` is a DSL expression evaluated with `{ value }` in scope, so
> `floor((score - 10) / 2)` is a content decision rather than an engine constant.


**L48–51**

> A depletable pool such as hit points or focus. Generalised so the engine never mentions HP: a
> module that wants stamina, sanity or heat adds a resource.


**L57**

> Evaluated with the character in scope, e.g. class die + endurance per level.


**L60**

> Starting value; defaults to full.


**L62**

> Fraction of max restored by a rest, 0..1.


**L65**

> Reaching min triggers these, which is how death is expressed.


**L70**

> A stat computed from attributes and gear, e.g. Guard, Initiative, Speed.


**L89**

> How a second application of the same condition behaves.


**L97**

> Which way a roll leans. Named once: conditions and abilities both use it.


**L107**

> Omitted means it lasts until removed.


**L110**

> Runs once per round; this is how poison and bleeding tick.


**L113**

> Additive modifiers to derived stats while active.


**L115**

> Actions the condition forbids, e.g. `stunned` blocking `action`.


**L117–125**

> Senses this shuts off, e.g. `blinded` closing `sight`. The sense's own `ignores` is the
> exception, which is what blindsight is; neither half means anything without the other.
>
> A bare id rather than a `ref`, matching `ignores` on the other side. A checked reference
> would make the composer's `damage` section depend on `movement`, which already reaches back
> to `damage` through `skills`. The semantic rules check the link instead; see
> `diagnostics/rules.ts`.


**L127–137**

> Which way the dice lean while this condition is applied.
>
> Four scopes: the bearer's attacks, attacks against them, their ability checks, and their
> saving throws. Every swing that applies is collected and reconciled by
> `rules.resolution.swingStacking`.
>
> Not a substitute for `modifiers`, and worth choosing between: a penalty to a defence shifts
> the mean and leaves the critical rate alone, while a swing changes the spread and moves
> criticals with it.


**L147–150**

> Whether being under this hides who you are, so a witness is less likely to name you. Typed
> rather than a tag the engine recognises by name.


**L152**

> A save each round to shake it off.


**L157**

> When the save is made: on application, at end of turn, or both.


**L162**

> Conditions this one supersedes, e.g. unconscious implying prone.


**L168–171**

> How a check resolves. Written as dice notation, so advantage is `2d20kh1` rather than an engine
> special case.


**L177–186**

> What happens when a roll is handed more than one swing.
>
> `cancel` is the common table reading: any advantage and any disadvantage together leave
> neither, and neither stacks past one. `net` goes by the sign of the count, so three
> advantages against one disadvantage still swing up.
>
> There is no `stack`: `advantageDice` is a single notation, so two levels of advantage cannot
> be named.


**L188**

> Natural roll at or above this is a critical success; null disables crits.


**L191–195**

> Which kinds of roll a critical and a fumble can happen on. `criticalSuccessAt` is all or
> nothing; this is the narrower dial. The default lists all three, so with `check` in the list
> a natural 20 picks a lock no amount of skill could open.


**L199–203**

> What a critical hit multiplies damage by: the whole amount, modifier included, not the dice
> alone. A damage op carries a rolled number rather than the expression that produced it, so
> the tabletop convention of doubling only the dice is not available here.


**L205–208**

> What a successful save against `onSuccess: "half"` leaves, as a fraction. The mirror of
> `criticalDamageMultiplier`.


**L210–213**

> The floor a passive score is measured from: `passiveBase + modifier`. A `3d6` ruleset where
> 10 is the mean rather than a floor needs a different number.


**L215–218**

> What "opposed" means when one side is only resisting. `passive` measures against `passiveBase
> + modifier`; `contested` has the other side roll too.


**L220–223**

> Which way a scaled damage number is rounded — resistance, a save for half, a critical. At
> `round`, seven halved is four; at `floor`, three.


**L225–228**

> Which way a reputation spill is rounded. Under `trunc` a relation weight of 0.4 on a ±2 deed
> spills nothing.


**L230–238**

> What a weapon attack adds, when the ruleset wants more than the bare attribute modifier.
>
> `actor.attackMod` is the modifier for whichever attribute the attack resolved to, and
> `actor.proficiency` is the module's own curve. Omitted, an attack adds the attribute modifier
> alone, so a weapon never improves with level.
>
> The mirror of `spellcasting.attackBonus`.


**L241**

> Named difficulties content can refer to instead of raw numbers.


**L250**

> Applied on reaching this level.


**L255–266**

> What a level beyond the first adds to the vital resource, on top of `rules.resources[].max`.
>
> - `roll` — roll the die.
> - `average` — its mean, rounded up.
> - `max` — the top face.
> - `none` — add nothing, leaving `resources[].max` to do the whole job.
>
> `die` chooses whose die: the class's, or the creature's size, which is what
> `rules.sizes[].hitDie` feeds. `bonus` is an expression over the character, so "constitution
> modifier per level" is expressible.


**L279**

> Proficiency-style bonus by level, if the module uses one.


**L281–284**

> The rank a bare `skillProficiencies` entry grants. Without it, training meant exactly 1 while
> `ancestry.skillBonuses` carried arbitrary numbers.


**L294**

> What a turn is made of.


**L300**

> Uses available per turn.


**L310**

> In world-clock minutes.


**L312**

> Which restore fraction on resources applies.


**L315**

> Chance of being interrupted while in a dangerous place.


**L320–323**

> A named rung on the skill ladder — novice, adept, master. Lets loot and gates ask for competence
> rather than a bare rank number.


**L329**

> Skill rank at which this tier is reached.


**L334**

> Equipment slots, so gear layout is a module decision.


**L335–338**

> A named quality a weapon can have: finesse, versatile, two-handed, thrown. Declaring the
> vocabulary means a property can carry modifiers and a requirement can ask for one.


**L344**

> Additive modifiers to derived stats while a weapon with this is wielded.


**L346–355**

> Attributes a weapon with this property may attack with, on top of the one the ability names.
> The best of them is used, for the attack roll and for the weapon's damage.
>
> This is what finesse is: a choice of which attribute the roll uses, which `modifiers` cannot
> express.
>
> It belongs to the property rather than the ability, so the weapon decides and one authored
> `strike` serves everyone.


**L361–364**

> What money is called here. The engine keeps a single scalar purse; this is what to print beside
> it.


**L365–368**

> How far the party can reach without moving. Combat reach comes from `sizes[].reach`; talking,
> picking things up and handing something over are set here.


**L371**

> Chebyshev tiles you can hold a conversation across.


**L373**

> Tiles you can pick something up from, or hand it over.


**L378–381**

> Searching a room, and defusing what it turns up: how much of a room one `search` covers, and
> whether disarming means standing on the thing.


**L384**

> How far a search reaches.


**L386**

> How close you must be to disarm what you found.


**L395–397**

> Whether the purse can go below zero. Off, the engine clamps and a debt cannot be expressed.


**L406**

> How many items fit, e.g. two rings.


**L426**

> The tactical layer. Each of these is an empty list by default, so a module takes on only the


**L427**

> complexity it wants.


**L440–443**

> What creatures do when nothing is telling them what to do. Beside `perception`, which decides
> what a creature can tell is there. Per-creature `temperament` overrides any of it.


**L445**

> Resource consumed when a character is reduced to zero, e.g. `hp`.


**L448**

> Attribute that breaks initiative ties, if any.


**L450**

> Default size for creatures that do not declare one.


**L452–456**

> How a creature gets about when it declares no speeds of its own, and how generation decides a
> map is connected. Without it the engine took the mode named `walk`, or else whichever was
> declared first.


**L460–464**

> How an NPC's signed `disposition` becomes a stance toward the party. Bands are matched
> highest `atLeast` first. The default cuts at zero and offers two stances; adding a band above
> it is how an NPC can spawn as an ally.


**L470**

> Lowest disposition in this band. Omit for the catch-all below them all.


### packages/module/src/schema/space.ts


**L1–10**

> Space: terrain, maps, and position.
>
> The game is played on a grid at all times. Everything here is declared by the module — a terrain
> is impassable because the module says `passable: false`.
>
> The unit is a tile. `rules.sizes` gives each creature size a `space` in whatever unit the module
> uses, and ability `range` and `areaOfEffect.size` use the same unit. Conversion happens once, at
> the edge.


**L16–19**

> A kind of ground. `passable` and `opaque` are separate: a chasm blocks movement but not sight, a
> curtain blocks sight but not movement.


**L27**

> Single character used to draw it on a text map.


**L30–34**

> Suggested colour when drawn on a text map. A hint for a front end, never a rule: the glyph
> alone still has to distinguish this terrain. A front end with no colour for a terrain falls
> back to its `tags`.


**L40**

> Blocks line of sight, and therefore field of view and witnessing.


**L43–46**

> Tiles of movement consumed to enter. 2 is the usual difficult terrain. Combined
> multiplicatively with the mover's `movementMode.terrainMultiplier`.


**L49**

> Only creatures with one of these movement modes may enter.


**L52**

> Cover granted to a creature standing behind this.


**L55**

> Fires when a creature enters — lava, caltrops, a pressure plate.


**L57**

> Fires at the end of a turn spent standing here.


**L60**

> Light emitted, in tiles. Zero is dark.


**L63–67**

> How well this ground keeps a trace, per sense. Zero keeps none. A fact about the ground
> rather than the creature standing on it, which is why it lives here and not on the sense.
> Absent is one.


**L70**

> A door-like terrain that a gate can be attached to.


**L76–79**

> How a space is drawn. A palette maps generator roles to terrain, so one generator produces a
> stone crypt or a reed marsh depending on which palette the biome names.


**L87–91**

> Terrain laid over the floor, either sprinkled or in patches. `speckle` rolls each tile on its
> own, which is right for debris; `patch` thresholds a noise field, so the terrain forms
> connected bodies with ragged edges and can be given a shore.


**L97**

> Fraction of the open floor this covers.


**L100**

> `patch` only: tiles across a typical blob.


**L102**

> `patch` only: 1 gives smooth outlines, 3 a ragged coast.


**L104**

> Laid in a band around a patch — a shore, a verge, a scree slope.


**L107**

> Lower is laid first, and a later entry never overwrites it.


**L113**

> Fills the space beyond the walls, e.g. rock, void, open sky.


**L119**

> A position on a map.


**L127–136**

> How a space is sized and drawn.
>
> Three ways to get a map, in precedence order: `static` names a hand-built `world.maps` entry used
> verbatim; `layout` is the older glyph-row escape hatch; otherwise the map is generated from
> `width` × `height` and the palette.
>
> The inner object is exported separately because `.superRefine` produces a ZodEffects, which
> cannot be `.extend`ed — anything composing this spec starts from `mapSpecObject`.


**L139**

> Dice notation, so size varies per instance.


**L143**

> A hand-built map used exactly as authored. Wins over `layout` and generation.


**L145–148**

> Hand-authored tiles, as rows of palette glyphs. When present, `width` and `height` are
> ignored.


**L150**

> Maps a glyph in `layout` to a terrain, beyond the palette's own.


**L159**

> A ragged layout would silently produce a jagged map; rows must be equal.


### packages/module/src/schema/staticmap.ts


**L1–15**

> Static maps: hand-authored, identical across seeds.
>
> A generated map answers "what might be here"; a static map answers "this is here". Castles, shops
> and set-piece rooms are authored as a grid of ids — one id per cell, one grid per layer — and the
> engine uses them verbatim.
>
> On disk a static map is a folder (`maps/<id>/` beside `module.json`): a `map.json` manifest plus
> one CSV per layer, assembled into this shape at load. In the assembled document the grids are
> plain 2-D arrays, which is what makes the ordinary machinery work unchanged — `collectRefs`
> proves every cell resolves, `hashModule` hashes the content, and `extends` merges maps by id.
>
> Layers are ordered by draw order, and the kind of a layer decides which collection its cells
> resolve against.


**L20–23**

> Which collection each layer kind's cells resolve against. `markers` is null: marker ids are free
> names ("entry", "door", "spawn") rather than references.


**L38**

> A grid whose non-empty cells must resolve in `target`.


**L47**

> Names the layer in the editor and its CSV file on disk.


**L55–59**

> A plain union rather than a discriminated one, deliberately: the compiler's `collectRefs` walks
> `ZodUnion` by descending the first branch that parses, which is what turns every non-empty cell
> into a checked reference. Each branch's `kind` literal makes the match unambiguous.


**L79–83**

> The marker id the party arrives at. Required (via lint) when an area, POI or dungeon uses
> this map directly; a map only ever stamped into a generated dungeon as a room needs `door`
> markers instead.


**L90**

> Every layer must be the same rectangle — a sparse layer is authored as empty cells, never as


**L91**

> missing rows.


**L117**

> The base terrain layer makes the tiles total; without one there is no ground to stand on.


### packages/module/src/schema/systemText.ts


**L1–23**

> Everything the engine says out loud.
>
> A stable key for every sentence, with the canonical wording as data rather than as code. The
> engine emits a key and its values; the module decides the words.
>
> Two tiers
>
> - `fragment` — a piece another message interpolates. `combat.outcome.failure` is the word
>   `{outcome}` in `combat.attacked`; delete it and the sentence has a hole where its verb was.
>   These are required in the document and a module that omits one does not compile.
> - `message` — a sentence that stands on its own. These carry a schema default, so an author
>   writes only what they want to change.
>
> A value is mandatory only when something else relies on it to convey a message.
>
> `placeholders` lists the `{names}` a message cannot lose — the ones carrying the facts. Others
> (`type`, `resisted`) are optional colour the engine supplies. `compileModule` checks both, so the
> failure is a load error.
>
> A value may also be `{ "pool": "some_pool" }` instead of a string, which hands the message to
> `narrative.textGrammar` for weighted, condition-gated variation.


**L29**

> Stable, dotted, and part of the module format's contract.


**L31**

> What the message is for, published in the generated reference.


**L33–36**

> `fragment` — interpolated into another message; required in the document. `message` — stands
> alone; defaulted, so it may be omitted.


**L38**

> `{names}` the text must keep. Losing one leaves a hole in the sentence.


**L40**

> The canonical English, seeded into a module by `npm run systemtext`.


**L44–47**

> Generic in the key so the literal survives into `SystemTextKey`, which makes a typo in an engine
> call site a type error rather than a message that never renders.


**L56–59**

> Every message the engine can produce, grouped the way a player meets them. Adding one here is the
> only way to add engine prose; the guard test in `spine.test.ts` fails on a literal.


**L61**

> — grammar ————————————————————————————————————————————————


**L86**

> — direction ——————————————————————————————————————————————


**L98**

> — rolls ——————————————————————————————————————————————————


**L103**

> — combat —————————————————————————————————————————————————


**L129**

> — movement ———————————————————————————————————————————————


**L133**

> — items and coin —————————————————————————————————————————


**L142**

> — traps and discovery ————————————————————————————————————


**L148**

> — progression ————————————————————————————————————————————


**L152**

> — gates ——————————————————————————————————————————————————


**L159**

> — requirements, as a player reads them ———————————————————


**L167**

> — lore ———————————————————————————————————————————————————


**L171**

> — quests —————————————————————————————————————————————————


**L185**

> — the party ——————————————————————————————————————————————


**L190**

> — the world ——————————————————————————————————————————————


**L202**

> — perception ——————————————————————————————————————————————


**L213**

> — looking around —————————————————————————————————————————


**L220**

> — refusals: acting at all ————————————————————————————————


**L226**

> — refusals: moving ———————————————————————————————————————


**L243**

> — refusals: fighting —————————————————————————————————————


**L264**

> — refusals: casting ——————————————————————————————————————


**L271**

> — refusals: things and people ————————————————————————————


**L293**

> — refusals: talking and trading ——————————————————————————


**L308**

> — what a button says when it will not work ———————————————


**L312**

> — refusals: quests ———————————————————————————————————————


**L317**

> — refusals the engine should never need ——————————————————


**L324**

> Every key the engine can ask for. A typo is a type error at the call site.


**L327**

> Lookup by key, built once.


**L332**

> Keys a document must declare: the pieces other messages are built from.


**L337**

> The canonical wording, ready to write into a module document.


**L344–347**

> Just the fragments — the smallest `systemText` block a module can compile with, so a fresh
> scaffold is a working document rather than a wall of prose.


**L354–357**

> One message: a string, or a text-grammar pool for weighted variation. The pool form is a `ref`,
> so a typo is a dangling reference at load.


**L363–367**

> What the engine says, keyed. Fragments are declared optional here so a module missing one gets
> `compileModule`'s diagnostic — which names the key and quotes the canonical wording — rather than
> Zod's bare "required".


### packages/module/src/schema/tactical.ts


**L1–8**

> The tactical layer: saving throws, resistances, spell slots, concentration, size and reach,
> senses, movement modes, opportunity attacks.
>
> None of it is hardcoded — each is a declared list a module fills in, so a game can have three
> saving throws or nine, spell slots or spell points or neither. If a rulebook would list it as a
> table, it belongs here as data.


**L14–17**

> A saving throw. Declared in data, so a module can ship the six D&D saves, Fortitude/Reflex/Will,
> or none.


**L24**

> Difficulty when nothing sets one explicitly.


**L30**

> A creature size, carrying the numbers that depend on it.


**L35**

> Space occupied, in feet or whatever unit the module uses.


**L37**

> Natural reach before weapons.


**L39**

> Multiplier on carrying capacity.


**L41**

> Hit-die step, for modules that scale HP by size.


**L47**

> Beast, undead, aberration — used by spells and features that target a type.


**L58–64**

> A way of noticing things: sight, hearing, smell, tremorsense.
>
> Every sense answers one question — how strong is the signal here — as a number from zero to one.
> The thresholds below carve that into bands, which separates noticing something from going to look
> at it from attacking it, and lets a cold trail be faint at arm's length.


**L70**

> Default range when a creature has it without one specified.


**L72**

> Ignores these conditions when perceiving, e.g. blindsight ignoring darkness.


**L75–79**

> `line` travels straight and needs an unobstructed path — sight, and sound. `field` seeps
> around corners through open space, which is how a smell reaches down a corridor you cannot
> see along.


**L82–85**

> What stops it. `opaque` blocks sight; `impassable` blocks everything else, so a reed bed or
> an open doorway hides you from view while carrying your noise and scent.


**L88–91**

> `cliff` is full strength to the edge of range and nothing beyond. `linear` fades with
> distance, so how close something is decides what a creature does about it.


**L94–97**

> Minutes a trace left on the ground stays perceptible. Zero leaves none, which is right for
> sight.


**L100–108**

> How fast this sense travels, in tiles per minute. Zero arrives at once.
>
> A speed, not a bonus to reach: a signal has only got `spreadPerMinute` tiles from its source
> for each minute since, so a creature further out cannot perceive it yet. Sight and sound
> leave this at zero.
>
> It is also why an old trace can reach an observer while a fresh one beside its owner has not.


**L110–114**

> Signal left at the far edge of reach, once it has spread that far. Eased from full strength
> at the source to this at the limit of the sense, rather than switching over at the first
> tile.


**L117–120**

> Minutes a creature remembers having perceived something. Zero forgets at once, which is right
> for sight.


**L123–127**

> Text grammar keys for what noticing something with this sense reads like: one for a strong
> impression, one for a faint or stale one. Without them the engine falls back to a plain line.
> `{direction}` is interpolated with where it lies.


**L130–133**

> What stopping to use this sense reads like when it turns nothing up. There is always a line,
> since silence reads as a broken command; this is how a module writes its own.


**L136**

> Signal needed to notice at all, to go and look, and to fight. Ordered.


**L154–158**

> How a creature is moving, and therefore what it gives off. A stance sets what you emit to each
> sense and a declared skill shifts it; there is no roll, so the same approach always sounds the
> same.


**L164**

> Multiplies movement allowance: creeping is slow, running is not.


**L166**

> What this stance gives off to each sense, as a multiplier. Absent means one.


**L168**

> Skill whose ranks quieten this stance further.


**L170**

> Emission removed per rank of that skill.


**L176–179**

> How perception behaves overall: which sense draws the map, and how long a creature stays curious
> about something it noticed and lost.


**L182**

> The sense used to draw the map and describe a room.


**L184**

> How long a creature keeps looking for something it noticed.


**L186**

> Stance a creature uses when it has chosen none.


**L188–191**

> The least a creature can ever give off, whatever its stance or skill. Above zero, perfect
> stealth is impossible.


**L194–202**

> How many traces one tile keeps, per sense; the oldest fall off.
>
> A small number rather than one, because two creatures passing in opposite directions is
> genuinely two things.
>
> A performance bound as much as a storage one: every trace on every tile is read for every
> sense, for every creature, every turn.


**L208–215**

> What a creature does when nobody is telling it what to do.
>
> Perception answers what it can tell is there; this answers what it does about it.
>
> Every default reproduces the engine's older behaviour: nothing wanders, nothing is leashed, and a
> fight ends the moment nobody can be perceived.


**L218–221**

> How far from where it was placed it will wander, in module units. Zero is a creature that
> stands where it was put, which is the default.


**L224–227**

> How far from that same spot a lead may pull it. Absent is no limit. Separate from
> `roamRadius` because catching a scent is a reason to leave its own ground.


**L230–233**

> How far it will chase before turning for home. Absent is no limit. Gates pursuit only: a
> leashed creature with something in reach still fights.


**L236**

> Odds it moves at all on a given idle step. Zero never wanders.


**L239–242**

> Rounds it stays in a fight after nobody can perceive anybody. Zero ends the fight at the end
> of the round somebody breaks away; one or two makes a corner a tactic rather than an exit.


**L245–248**

> Multipliers on how fast it moves for each reason it moves. Zero never moves that way at all:
> a shopkeeper sets `wander` to zero and stays behind the counter.


**L259–262**

> Which senses it acts on, best first. Absent means all of them, strongest signal first. An
> empty list notices everything and investigates none of it. Order is preference, not strength.


**L265–268**

> Whether a trace left on the ground is worth following. False is a creature that acts on what
> is there now, never on what passed through an hour ago.


**L271–274**

> Whose presence it registers at all. Defaults to enemies only, which is the filter perception
> applied with no way to say otherwise. Widening it is what lets creatures notice each other.


**L280–287**

> The same thing as a per-creature override, where every field is genuinely absent rather than
> defaulted.
>
> Written out rather than derived with `.partial()`, which is shallow: that would leave `speeds`
> carrying its inner defaults, so a creature overriding only its wander speed would silently reset
> the others to one.


**L311**

> Walk, fly, swim, burrow, climb.


**L316**

> Speed when a creature has the mode but no explicit rate.


**L318**

> Difficult terrain and similar costs.


**L320**

> Falls when the mode is lost, e.g. flight while unconscious.


**L332**

> Rare languages gate dialogue and readable content.


**L347–350**

> How damage of a type is modified for a creature. A single multiplier covers resistance (0.5),
> immunity (0), vulnerability (2) and healing from damage (-1).


**L355**

> Ignored when the damage carries one of these tags, e.g. silvered.


**L361–364**

> Spellcasting. `mode` chooses the economy: Vancian slots, a points pool, both, or neither. A
> module using only the Focus pool leaves this out.


**L368**

> Highest spell level the module uses.


**L370**

> Slots per caster level: `slotTable["3"] = [4, 2]` is four 1st and two 2nd.


**L372**

> Resource spent when `mode` includes points.


**L374**

> Point cost per spell level, indexed by level.


**L376**

> Spell save DC and attack bonus, as formulas over the caster.


**L379**

> Casting again while concentrating drops the first spell.


**L383**

> Save made when damaged while concentrating.


**L385**

> Usually max(10, half the damage taken).


**L387**

> Concentrating on more than one spell at a time.


**L392**

> Recovered on which rest kinds.


**L395–398**

> Which action a spell component is, so silence and manacles work. Refs rather than the engine
> looking for action types named `speak` and `gesture`, so a wrong id is a load error.


**L410–413**

> Reactions triggered by someone else's action — opportunity attacks, shield, counterspell.
> Declared rather than built in, so a module can remove them.


**L419**

> What provokes it.


**L424**

> Action type it consumes, usually a reaction.


**L426**

> Ability used to respond; omit for a plain weapon attack.


**L430**

> Uses per round, across all provocations.


**L436**

> Cover, and anything else that adjusts a roll situationally.


**L441**

> Bonus to the defender's guard-equivalent.


**L443**

> Blocks line of effect entirely.


### packages/module/src/schema/walk.ts


**L1–16**

> One walk of the module schemas, shared by everything that describes them.
>
> The reference docs, the field-gloss coverage test and the documentation site all read the same
> walk of the real Zod schemas, so whatever the validator enforces is what they show.
>
> Two things handled once:
>
> - Shared schemas are documented once. `requirement` appears on some thirty entities, and
>   recursing into it every time runs the reference to fifteen thousand lines. Identity is the
>   schema object itself, which Zod shares between use sites, and the first path that reaches one
>   wins. A path is claimed as the child is scanned, not as it is visited, so two sibling fields
>   sharing a schema both link to the same section.
> - Types are returned as a small tree, not as text, so the reference can render Markdown and the
>   site elements without either reimplementing the other.


**L29–34**

> Deepest section the walk emits, counting the document itself as 0: `content` is 1,
> `content.abilities` 2, `content.abilities.requires` 3, `content.abilities.requires.attributes` 4.
> The bound keeps a cyclic schema from becoming an infinite document; a field below it still gets a
> row, rendered as a plain object with no section to link to.


**L37**

> The section rendered from the `SYSTEM_TEXT` registry rather than the shape.


**L40**

> The four shapes the behaviour language takes, named rather than lumped.


**L43**

> A field's type, shaped so Markdown and JSX can each render it their own way.


**L57–64**

> Schemas that mean something specific and would otherwise read as `string` or as one
> undifferentiated union.
>
> Matched by object identity, checked at every step of the optional/default unwrap, because
> `EffectSchema` reached through `z.array(...).default([])` is the same object. `ref()` calls clone
> `idSchema` through `.describe()`, so a reference field never matches the bare id entry.


**L80**

> Canonical dotted path, e.g. `content.npcs.gullibility`. Unique.


**L85**

> The schema default, or `undefined` when the field has none.


**L87**

> Collection this field points at, when it is a reference.


**L89**

> The sentence a reference field carries after its collection, if any.


**L91**

> Section path this field's own fields live under, when it is an object.


**L96**

> Dotted path, `''` for the document itself.


**L98**

> Owning section path, `null` for the document itself.


**L100**

> `content.npcs.shop` reads as `['content', 'npcs', 'shop']`.


**L103–107**

> True for `narrative.systemText`, whose two hundred keys come from the `SYSTEM_TEXT` registry.
> The registry carries the tier, the placeholders a message may not drop, and a description per
> key, none of which live in the Zod type.


**L116**

> Set when the chain passed through a schema in `NAMED`.


**L120**

> Strip `optional`/`nullable`/`default`/`effects` down to the real type.


**L154**

> The object branches of a union, in declaration order.


**L164–168**

> The object a field is built from, looking through arrays and unions. A union contributes its
> first object branch only, which covers both unions the format has: static map layers, whose
> branches differ only in the `kind` literal, and a class's skill proficiencies.


**L184**

> Object schema to the first path that reached it.


**L219**

> Every branch an object, all the same shape bar a discriminating literal: one section says


**L220**

> it, and the variant count says the rest.


**L231**

> A lazy schema that is not one of the four named DSL shapes.


**L258–262**

> Claim a section for an object field as it is scanned, so two siblings sharing one schema
> resolve to the same section. `extra` is an open bag with no shape worth tabulating, so it
> stays a plain record row.


**L296**

> Every field path the walk produces, in document order. The coverage set.


**L301**

> Sections grouped by their top-level part; the document itself keys as `''`.


### packages/module/src/schema/world.ts


**L1–14**

> The world: where things are, what happens when you go there, and what stops you getting in.
>
> The spatial hierarchy is Biome → Area → Point of Interest:
>
>   - a biome is a theme supplying room templates, encounter tables and ambience;
>   - an area is a named place on the map, belonging to a biome, connected to other areas, and
>     holding points of interest;
>   - a point of interest is somewhere you go: a shrine, a locked door, a camp, a dungeon mouth, a
>     market.
>
> Procedural dungeons hang off a point of interest rather than replacing it, so an authored world
> and a generated one compose.


**L23–26**

> How an automatic event behaves on repeat visits: a set piece that fires once, an ambush that can
> happen every time, or a ritual that resets if you leave it unfinished.


**L35–38**

> An automatic event. `remember` writes the fact that this fired to the world, so it survives
> leaving and returning and a ransacked shrine stays ransacked.


**L44**

> Who or what sets it off.


**L48**

> Custom event name, when `on` is `custom`.


**L50**

> Gate on party state, memory, faction standing, and so on.


**L52**

> Extra condition beyond the requirement.


**L54**

> For `loop`: in-world minutes before it can fire again.


**L56**

> For `untilComplete`: what counts as done.


**L58**

> Persist that this fired, so the world remembers between visits.


**L60**

> Probability it fires at all, for ambient events.


**L63**

> Prose pool to narrate when it fires.


**L68–71**

> A barrier. Locked doors, wards, riddles and tolls are the same shape: a requirement that opens
> it, an optional roll to force it, and what happens either way.


**L79**

> Meeting this opens it outright — the key, the password, the rank.


**L81**

> Forcing it: a skill check, for when the party lacks the requirement.


**L86**

> Failing badly enough may spring a trap or raise an alarm.


**L89**

> Whether a failed attempt can be retried.


**L94**

> A magical opening: casting the right ability rather than turning a key.


**L97**

> Shown and run when the party is turned away.


**L100**

> Stays open once opened, rather than relocking behind you.


**L105**

> What a room can be, before the generator fills it in.


**L121**

> Override the trap roll entirely, rather than letting the room's `role` decide.


**L127**

> Shape and size of the room's tiles.


**L138**

> Scale the count with party level, for encounters that stay relevant.


**L143–147**

> One possible encounter, with its odds. `weight` sets relative probability within the table;
> `requires` removes the group from the draw entirely when the party does not qualify, which keeps
> a table level-appropriate without separate tables per tier.


**L155**

> Fires when this group is rolled, before combat begins.


**L158**

> Not every encounter is a fight.


**L170**

> Odds that any encounter happens when this table is consulted.


**L172**

> Nothing happened: the weight given to a quiet result.


**L174–177**

> Levels per extra creature for entries that scale. The engine's fixed rule was `floor((level -
> 1) / 2)`.


**L184**

> A themed environment: which rooms, monsters, and loot belong together.


**L197**

> Applies to every area in this biome, so weather and dread are set once.


**L199**

> Default palette for anything generated in this biome.


**L205**

> What kind of place a point of interest is.


**L219–222**

> Somewhere the party can go. A point of interest carries its own triggers, encounters, gate and
> inhabitants, so the behaviour of a place lives with the place.


**L233**

> Barred until the gate opens.


**L235**

> Hidden until found, by a check or by a trigger.


**L244**

> Odds of an encounter per visit here, overriding the table's own.


**L249**

> Entering leads into a generated dungeon.


**L251**

> Settlements: what can be done here.


**L253**

> How fast news spreads from here, as a multiplier on rumour reach.


**L256**

> In-world minutes to reach from the area's centre.


**L258**

> Where it sits on the area's map. Generated when absent.


**L260**

> The interior, for places you walk around inside.


**L266**

> A named place on the map.


**L277**

> Neighbours and what it costs to walk there.


**L284**

> A pass that must be opened before the route is usable.


**L286**

> One-way routes: a cliff you can descend but not climb.


**L298–301**

> Odds of a wandering encounter on entering, as an expression over `dangerLevel`. The default
> is `min(0.75, danger * 0.15)`.


**L305**

> Suggested party level, used by the editor's balance warnings.


**L308**

> The walkable map for this area.


**L310**

> Where the party arrives. Generated when absent.


**L316**

> Parameters the dungeon generator reads.


**L327**

> Gates the generator may place on interior doors.


**L330**

> Whether the room the party arrives in is quiet — no encounter, no trap.


**L334**

> Overrides the biome's palette for this dungeon.


**L336–339**

> Tiles of corridor between rooms. Its mean sets the spacing rooms are placed at; `winding`
> corridors also read each edge's roll as a minimum path length.


**L341–344**

> How a `winding` corridor wanders: the chance it carries straight on, and how much a turn is
> penalised when routing.


**L352**

> Room size when a template declares no map spec of its own.


**L354–357**

> How the dungeon is made: `rooms` and corridors, `bsp` (dense building-like interiors, rooms
> sharing walls), or `caverns` (organic cellular caves — no doors, so no locks or branchiness).


**L359–362**

> Tuning for `bsp`: the smallest leaf the space is cut into, which decides how fine-grained the
> interior reads.


**L364–368**

> Tuning for `caverns`, the cellular-automaton dials: `fill` is how much rock the noise starts
> as, `smoothingPasses` how many rounds it is eroded, and `birthThreshold` how many rock
> neighbours make rock.


**L377–380**

> A hand-built `world.maps` entry used as the whole dungeon, identical across seeds. Generation
> is skipped; the map's own layers place its gates, traps, items and creatures.


**L382–385**

> Static dungeons only: roll the biome's encounter tables once per `spawn` marker on the map,
> so a fixed castle can still have variable guards.


**L387**

> What the passages are like.


**L390**

> `l` elbows, `straight` runs, or `winding` burrows.


**L392**

> Tiles wide. Doors stay one tile regardless.


**L397**

> Map bounds, dice notation. Derived from `roomCount` when absent.


**L404**

> The world clock.


**L408–411**

> How long an hour is, so a calendar of hundred-minute hours is expressible alongside
> `minutesPerDay`.


**L421**

> What an action costs in world-clock minutes, by action id. Absent entries cost nothing.


**L428**

> Minutes spent crossing one tile out of combat. Zero, the default, means walking is free.


**L444–448**

> Hand-authored maps, identical across seeds. Authored as folders of CSV layers beside
> `module.json` (`maps/<id>/`) and inlined here at load; inline entries are also legal, which
> is what a browser export produces.


**L451–454**

> What generation does for a room that names no template. Matches `roomTemplates`' own

## `packages/module/src`

_291 comments_


### packages/module/src/staticmaps.test.ts


**L1–8**

> The static map file form: CSV grids, assembly, and its inverse.
>
> The property that matters most is at the bottom: a module whose maps are
> authored inline hashes identically to the same module with those maps in
> folders. Everything else — trimming, comments, error positions — exists so
> an author editing a CSV by hand gets told exactly where they went wrong.


**L134**

> File names come from layer name, then kind; explicit in the manifest.


**L166**

> A valid map over the minimal module's own terrain ids.


**L226**

> Inline in one order, or "assembled from folders" in the other — after


**L227**

> normalization they are the same document, so the same hash. This is the


**L228**

> invariant that makes folder storage invisible to saves.


**L235**

> And a split/assemble round trip changes nothing at all.


### packages/module/src/staticmaps.ts


**L1–14**

> Static map files: the CSV-per-layer form and its assembly.
>
> A static map lives on disk as a folder — `maps/<id>/` beside `module.json` — holding a `map.json`
> manifest and one CSV per layer. In the assembled document it is an ordinary `world.maps` entry
> with the grids inlined. This module is the bridge in both directions, and is pure: the editor
> needs `splitStaticMap` in the browser, and `@dm/module`'s main index is bundled client-side.
> Filesystem policy lives in `load.ts`.
>
> CSV here is the trivial dialect: ids are `[a-z][a-z0-9_]*`, so no cell needs quoting and a parser
> is a split on commas. Cells are trimmed, an empty cell means nothing, and a line whose first non-
> space character is `#` is a comment. Anything irregular is an error with a line and column, never
> a guess.


**L17**

> 1-based line in the file as authored, comments counted.


**L19**

> 1-based column of the offending cell's first character.


**L26**

> The file the problem is in, relative to the map folder.


**L36**

> Parse one CSV layer file into a grid of trimmed cell ids.


**L41**

> One trailing newline ends a file rather than adding an empty final row; strip exactly one so


**L42**

> serialize ∘ parse is the identity.


**L53**

> A blank line is ambiguous — a row of empties or a stray newline — so it is refused rather


**L54**

> than interpreted.


**L96**

> The inverse of `parseCsvGrid`: no padding, one row per line, trailing newline.


**L101–108**

> Manifest plus CSV texts → an assembled `world.maps` entry.
>
> Structural rather than schema-validating: unknown manifest fields ride through untouched, so
> `extra` and future fields round-trip, and the zod check happens later at compile. The only errors
> raised here are the ones a schema can never see — a layer naming a file that is not in the
> folder, and CSV syntax.


**L179–184**

> An assembled entry → manifest plus CSV texts, the deterministic inverse. The written manifest
> always lists explicit `file` names — layer `name` when present, layer kind otherwise, suffixed
> with the layer index on collision — so a folder written twice from the same entry is byte-
> identical.


**L210–214**

> Normalize `world.maps` to id order. Assembly discovers folders in directory order and `extends`
> appends in merge order, so sorting afterwards makes the assembled document — and the module hash
> — a pure function of content.

## `packages/tools/src/bin`

_211 comments_


### packages/tools/src/bin/bench-validate.ts


**L1–16**

> `npm run bench -- modules/<name>`
>
> What one keystroke in the editor costs.
>
> The studio revalidates the whole document on every change, so the number this
> prints is the editor's frame budget rather than a CLI timing. It is here
> because the cost is dominated by one line — `gameModuleSchema.safeParse` — in
> a way that is invisible from the outside: on `modules/aurendel` the parse is
> ~610 ms and *everything else the compiler does* is single-digit milliseconds.
> Anyone optimising this file's other passes would be optimising noise.
>
> Deliberately not a test. A latency budget asserted in CI fails on a busy
> machine and teaches people to skip the suite; this is a thing you run when
> you have changed something and want to know which way it moved.


**L24**

> The editor's `setAt`, in miniature: copy the spine, share everything else.


**L38**

> Median of several runs: a single timing is mostly whatever else the CPU was doing.


**L88**

> What the editor actually pays once a session is warm: one field changed,


**L89**

> every other entry shared with the previous document.


**L120**

> What the store adds on top of the lint, for the raw JSON view and export.


**L128**

> Other whole-document walks the editor does per edit, which the parse cache


**L129**

> does nothing for.


### packages/tools/src/bin/build-content.ts


**L1–16**

> `npm run content`
>
> Emit the example worlds each app ships, pre-zipped, in the form each app can use.
>
> The player receives `<id>.json.gz`: the compiled `module.json`, which it reads and never edits.
> The studio receives `<id>.project.json.gz`: the project tree exactly as the repository holds it,
> recipes and prefabs and style tables included, because the studio edits project files.
>
> The repository keeps its modules pretty-printed for readable diffs. Aurendel is 2.9 MB pretty,
> 1.5 MB minified and 296 KB gzipped, so the readable form stays in git and this writes the small
> one at build time — there is no server to do it on.
>
> What ships is an explicit allowlist rather than the contents of `modules/`: `greenmarch` and
> `minimal` are test fixtures and are not games.

> the cwd set to the app being built, so `process.cwd()` would look for `apps/play/modules/` and
> find nothing.


**L33–37**

> What each app is allowed to ship. The player gets Aurendel; the studio gets it too, plus

### packages/tools/src/bin/check-content.ts


**L1–14**

> `npm run check:content`
>
> Prove the shipped examples still are what the repository says they are.
>
> The artifacts are generated, gitignored binaries, so nothing about them shows up in a diff —
> which makes "edit a world, forget to run `npm run content`" a mistake with no symptom until a
> deployment serves the old one. The committed `modules/content-manifest.json` carries the id,
> version, hash and size of every module that ships, so a content change is a readable one-line
> diff.
>
> This belongs inside `npm run check`: it shells out to nothing, takes under a second, and never
> writes to the working tree.

### packages/tools/src/bin/check-projects.ts


**L1–15**

> `npm run check:projects`
>
> Every TypeScript file must belong to a TypeScript project.
>
> A file that no `tsconfig.json` includes still opens fine in an editor — it
> lands in an *inferred* project, which has no path mappings, no Node types and
> default module resolution. It lights up red while `npm run typecheck` passes
> clean, because the typecheck never looked at it. That happened here to all
> twenty test files and to `vitest.config.ts`, and it hid a test calling a
> six-argument function with four.
>
> The check is the same one a person would do by hand: ask each project what
> files it sees, and compare that against what is on disk.

### packages/tools/src/bin/comments.ts


**L1–17**

> `npm run comments [-- <out>]`
>
> Every comment in the project, in one file, in folder order.
>
> Uses the TypeScript parser for TS/TSX/JS rather than a regex: a regex for a line comment matches
> every URL, and one for a block comment matches every such sequence inside a string. `prelude.ts`
> holds five commented lines inside the template literal it ships to QuickJS, and a site page
> renders two more as example code; all seven must be excluded. The other languages get small
> scanners, none of which has a regex literal.
>
> Python docstrings count, because in `dmkit` that is where the explanation is. They are found by
> position — a string opening a file or a `def`/`class` block — so a triple-quoted value is not
> mistaken for one.
>
> JSON is skipped: no comments, and 3,185 of the project's files.


**L25**

> 1-based, and inclusive of both ends.


**L31**

> Files whose comments are worth having, by extension.


**L35**

> Everything git tracks, which is the definition of "the project" that stays true.


**L42**

> Line starts, so a character offset can name a line.


**L60–64**

> Comments are trivia: they hang off the token that follows them, so every token in the tree is
> asked what precedes it. The end-of-file token is a child like any other, which catches a comment
> with nothing after it.


**L94**

> `#` to end of line, for YAML and shell. Quote-aware: a `#` in a string is not one.


**L119**

> `/* … *\/` only, quote-aware. CSS has no line comments and no regex literals.


**L144–148**

> A docstring is a string that opens something — the file, or the block after a `def` or `class`.
> That is a position rather than a shape, so a triple-quoted string assigned to a variable is left
> alone.


**L153**

> Does a docstring belong here? True at the top of the file and after a block opener.


**L197**

> A line ending in `:` opens a block, and the next statement in it may be a docstring. Anything


**L198**

> else means the block has started without one.


**L205**

> Comment markers off.


**L211**

> A `*` gutter is decoration; `*/` is not, hence the lookahead.

> Drop the indentation the whole block shares, keeping the shape inside it.


**L228**

**L255**

**L285**

**L312**

### packages/tools/src/bin/compress-project.ts


**L1–28**

> `npm run compress -- <module-dir> [--write]`
>
> Turn a project's entry files into recipes: a prefab id, a handful of parameters, and whatever the
> prefab did not reproduce.
>
> Aurendel's 597 points of interest are thirteen shapes filled in repeatedly — group them by
> description pool and 107 are houses, 61 stores, 45 inns — so storing each one whole writes the
> same eleven keys 597 times.
>
> How a prefab is found
>
> Per group, every field is sorted into one of three kinds:
>
>   - constant — one value across the whole group, so it belongs in the template and is written
>     once instead of N times;
>   - co-varying — several fields that move together over a small set of combinations, which
>     becomes a style table and a single `variant` parameter. This is where the compression is;
>   - free — genuinely per-entry, so a parameter.
>
> Nothing is inferred about meaning; this is arithmetic over the values that are there.
>
> Why it cannot produce a wrong module
>
> Every recipe is expanded again and compared to the entry it replaced, as serialized text so key
> order counts. An entry that does not round-trip keeps its literal file. The worst outcome is a
> project that did not get smaller.


**L36**

> Fields that are what an entry is, never a variant of something else.


**L39**

> Above this many combinations a "variant" stops being a vocabulary.


**L50–58**

> Entries that plainly came from one shape: the prose pool they share, and the order their keys are
> written in.
>
> The order half matters because a template emits keys in its own order and a rebuilt module has to
> match `module.json` byte for byte. It costs little — Aurendel's forty-five inns are written two
> ways and its hundred and seven houses five — so the split makes more prefabs rather than fewer
> recipes.


**L62**

> The pool when there is one, because it is the most legible name a generated prefab can have;


**L63**

> the key order always, because byte-identity depends on it.


**L71**

> Named for the pool, numbered only when one pool is written several ways.


**L79**

> Qualified by collection because prefabs share one flat folder and `expandRecipe` resolves


**L80**

> by id alone.


**L81**

> Lower-cased before stripping, or every capital is deleted and `world.pointsOfInterest`


**L82**

> comes out as `world_points_f_nterest`.


**L102**

> A field absent from some entries cannot be a template constant: the template would give it to


**L103**

> everyone.


**L111**

> A field can only belong to a vocabulary if it has one: `position` is different for every entry


**L112**

> and `area` takes fifty-five values, so including them would poison every combination they


**L113**

> appear in. Filtering on each field's own spread before looking at combinations is what makes


**L114**

> this find anything.


**L120**

> Then the largest subset of those that still moves together over few enough combinations.


**L121**

> Dropping the widest field first is what makes this converge.


**L135**

> Nothing to save: every field is either unique or already spelled out.


**L162**

> Template key order follows the entries' own, because a rebuilt module has to match


**L163**

> `module.json` byte for byte.


**L228**

> Expanded from the text rather than from the recipe object: an override whose value is


**L229**

> `undefined` survives in memory and suppresses the template's key, then vanishes in


**L230**

> `JSON.stringify`. The check has to see the bytes that will be on disk.


**L232**

> Serialized, so key order counts. Anything that does not survive keeps the literal file


**L233**

> it already had.


**L269**

> Only the prefabs that earned a recipe, and only the style rows they use.

### packages/tools/src/bin/free-port.ts


**L1–15**

> `tsx packages/tools/src/bin/free-port.ts 4400`
>
> Clear a stale dev server off a port before starting a new one.
>
> A dev server that outlives the terminal it was started from is invisible
> until the next `npm run editor`, which then fails with `EADDRINUSE` and a
> stack trace that says nothing about what to do. This finds the process on the
> port, checks it belongs to this repository, and stops it.
>
> The ownership check is the important part: it will never touch a process
> whose working directory is outside this checkout, so a database or another
> project's server happening to sit on the same port is reported rather than
> killed.


**L23**

> Process ids listening on a port, by whichever tool this machine has.


**L38**

> The command ran and found nothing — that is an answer, not a failure.


**L41**

> Not installed, or refused: try the next one.


**L47**

> Where a process was started from, or null if that cannot be read.


**L71**

> Give it a moment to close its listener, then insist.


**L85**

> Already gone between the check and the signal.


**L103**

> Someone else's process. Say so plainly and leave it alone — a port


**L104**

> collision is worth a sentence, not a kill.

>     npx tsx packages/tools/src/bin/playlore.ts --all --quiet
>
> A hidden thread has more ways to be silently broken than a side chain, because none of it is
> announced: a clue nobody can be asked for reads like a clue nobody has asked for yet, and an
> anchor whose discovery difficulty never comes down is a place that does not exist for the player.
>
> Three routes, and the thread must survive all three:
>
>   friendly — full standing, asks for everything, never draws a weapon
>   hostile  — standing driven below every floor, so nobody will talk; takes what it needs off the
> bodies
>   blind    — learns no clues at all, and searches until the anchor turns up
>
> The third tests the claim that a clue informs the search rather than gating it. If `blind` cannot
> reach the relic, the content is a lock wearing a rumour's clothes.
>
> Like `playchain`, this teleports and fakes kills. What is under test is the wiring, not the
> combat maths.
